Algebraic Bethe Ansatz for the Open XXZ Spin Chain with Non-Diagonal Boundary Terms via 𝑼 𝖖 ​ 𝖘 ​ 𝖑 𝟐 \boldsymbol{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}} Symmetry
Dmitry CHERNYAK ab , Azat M. GAINUTDINOV c , Jesper Lykke JACOBSEN abd and Hubert SALEUR be

Abstract
We derive by the traditional algebraic Bethe ansatz method the Bethe equations for the general open XXZ spin chain with non-diagonal boundary terms under the Nepomechie constraint [ J. Phys. A 37 (2004), 433–440, arXiv:hep-th/0304092]. The technical difficulties due to the breaking of 𝖴 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathsf{U}(1) symmetry and the absence of a reference state are overcome by an algebraic construction where the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb Hamiltonian is realised in a new U 𝔮 ​ 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -invariant spin chain involving infinite-dimensional Verma modules on the edges [ J. High Energy Phys. 2022 (2022), no. 11, 016, 64 pages, arXiv:2207.12772]. The equivalence of the two Hamiltonians is established by proving Schur–Weyl duality between U 𝔮 ​ 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} and the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra. In this framework, the Nepomechie condition turns out to have a simple algebraic interpretation in terms of quantum group fusion rules.



keywords
quantum integrable models; non-diagonal K-matrices; Verma modules; Temperley–Lieb algebras

81R50; 81R12; 81U15; 16T25

1
Introduction

Let us consider the open XXZ Hamiltonian acting on
\mathcal{H}:=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
with the most general boundary fields

\displaystyle H_{{\rm n.d.}}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\big(\sigma^{x}_{i}\sigma^{x}_{i+1}+\sigma^{y}_{i}\sigma^{y}_{i+1}+\cosh(h)\sigma^{z}_{i}\sigma^{z}_{i+1}+\sinh(h)\big(\sigma_{i+1}^{z}-\sigma_{i}^{z}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{H_{{\rm n.d.}} := }{}+\frac{\sinh(h)}{2\sinh(h\delta_l)\cosh(h\kappa_l)}\big({\rm e}^{h\theta_{l}}\sigma_{1}^{+}+{\rm e}^{-h\theta_{l}}\sigma_{1}^{-}+\sinh(h(\delta_l+\kappa_l))\sigma^{z}_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{H_{{\rm n.d.}} := }{}+\frac{\sinh(h)}{2\sinh(h\delta_r)\cosh(h\kappa_r)}\big({\rm e}^{h\theta_{r}}\sigma_{N}^{+}+{\rm e}^{-h\theta_{r}}\sigma_{N}^{-}-\sinh(h(\delta_r+\kappa_r))\sigma^{z}_{N}\big)

(1.1)

depending on
7
parameters
h
,
\delta_{l/r}
,
\kappa_{l/r}
and
\theta_{l/r}
, where

\sigma^{x}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma^{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-{\rm i}\\
{\rm i}&\hphantom{-}0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma^{z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\hphantom{-}0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}

are Pauli matrices and

\sigma^{+}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sigma^{x}+{\rm i}\sigma^{y}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma^{-}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sigma^{x}-{\rm i}\sigma^{y}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

are raising and lowering matrices. Performing a rotation of angle
\theta
around the
z
-axis shifts
\theta_{l/r}
by
\theta
while leaving the other
5
parameters unchanged so one can always set, for example,
\theta_{r}
to
0
. In other words, the spectrum of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
only depends on the difference

\Theta:=\theta_{l}-\theta_{r}

(1.2)

and so there is actually only
6
relevant parameters.
1
1

1

One can also show that the spectrum of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
does not depend on the sign of
\Theta
(see end of Section
2.1
).

While it is known from Sklyanin’s boundary Bethe ansatz formalism
[
58
]
and the subsequent construction of boundary
K
-matrices
[
21
,
22
]
that
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
is integrable, the rigorous implementation of this procedure for the most general choice of parameters is far from being straightforward. The main reason is that the non-diagonal boundary terms in (
1.1
) containing
\sigma_{1}^{\pm}
and
\sigma_{N}^{\pm}
break the
\mathsf{U}(1)
invariance of the usual XXZ model and so
\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes N}
is not an eigenvector of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
anymore and cannot be used as a reference state (also sometimes called “pseudovacuum”) for algebraic Bethe ansatz (ABA).

In the last twenty years, various new approaches have been proposed to circumvent this problem. A first major breakthrough was made in
[
53
,
54
]
and independently in
[
10
]
, with the derivation of the Bethe ansatz equations (BAE) under the assumption

\delta_{l}+\kappa_{l}+\delta_{r}+\kappa_{r}\pm\Theta=2M+1-N\in\mathbb{Z},

(1.3)

where
M\geq 0
is the magnon number, a constraint informally known as the ‘‘Nepomechie condition”.
2
2

2

Note that the notations
\alpha_{\pm}
,
\beta_{\pm}
,
\theta_{\pm}
,
\eta
in
[
54
]
correspond here to
\delta_{l/r}
,
\kappa_{l/r}
,
\theta_{l/r}
,
h
respectively and that
k=2M+1-N
according to
[
54
, equation (3.31)]
.
Later on, the BAE for any choice of parameters were derived
[
60
]
and it turned out that if the constraint (
1.3
) is not satisfied they contained an additional “inhomogeneous” term, which moreover fixed the magnon number to
M=N
(see more details in Appendix
C
). The same equations were also obtained using various forms of coordinate Bethe ansatz
[
16
,
20
,
57
]
and the separation of variables method
[
44
]
. Additionally, the closely related modified algebraic Bethe ansatz formalism was developed
[
2
,
6
,
7
,
8
]
as well as an algebraic framework based on the so-called
\mathfrak{q}
-Onsager algebra
[
5
]
.

Although these methods provide a satisfactory solution to the spectral problem of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
it is fair to say they are rather indirect and still lack a simple representation-theoretic understanding. Indeed, in the above-mentioned works, the BAE are either derived by analytically continuing some truncated functional relations and fusion rules at roots of unity
[
53
,
54
]
, by using an intricate dynamical gauge (or face-vertex
[
31
]
) transformation
[
8
,
10
]
or by writing the most general form for the eigenvalues of the transfer matrix respecting certain analyticity conditions and asymptotics
[
60
]
. In all these different approaches, the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
) naturally appears at some step of the computation but its representation-theoretic meaning remains elusive. Notice that the role of Nepomechie condition (
1.3
) was also touched upon from different perspectives in several other works
[
2
,
3
]
(see also
[
33
]
for the XXX case).

In this paper, we will rigorously derive the BAE for all
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
, under the constraint (
1.3
) by standard algebraic Bethe ansatz and explain the algebraic origin of this condition.

Our first step is to reinterpret
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
as (a representation of) an abstract element

\mathbf{H}:=-\mu_{l}b_{l}-\mu_{r}b_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}

belonging to a certain lattice algebra, namely the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
, evaluated in a specific
2^{N}
-dimensional representation
(\mathcal{W}_{0},\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}})
called the vacuum module (see definitions in Section
2.1
). Concretely, this means that, with some (explicit) mapping of parameters
(h,\delta_{l/r},\kappa_{l/r},\Theta)\leftrightarrow(\delta,y_{l/r},Y,\mu_{l/r})
, we have
{H_{{\rm n.d.}}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})}
(see Theorem
2.1
which is due to
[
19
]
).

The next step is to repackage all the
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
satisfying (
1.3
) into sectors of a different spin chain whose Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}_{2b}:=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}

is constructed from
N
spin-
\frac{1}{2}
representations and two infinite-dimensional Verma modules
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l/r}}
of the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
quantum group and whose Hamiltonian
H_{2b}
commutes with the action of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
on
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
(Sections
2.2
and
2.3
). As a representation of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
,
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
decomposes into an infinite direct sum of irreducible representations, with some multiplicity spaces
\mathcal{H}_{M}
,
M\geq 0
(
2.36
). It was shown in
[
14
]
3
3

3

The analysis in this reference was performed for even
N
only, however it can be extended rather straightforwardly to the odd
N
case too.
that
\mathcal{H}_{M}
carries an action of the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
for some explicit value
Y_{M}
of the
Y
parameter (
2.38
). Under explicit assumptions on the generic values of the bulk parameter
\mathfrak{q}
and of the boundary parameters
\alpha_{l/r}
we prove in Theorem
2.3
that
\mathcal{H}_{M}
, as a
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-module, is isomorphic to the (irreducible) vacuum module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
if
M\geq N
and to an irreducible piece of
\mathcal{W}_{0}
if
0\leq M\leq N-1
, thereby confirming
[
14
, Conjecture 1]
. This new Schur–Weyl duality between
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and the two-boundary Temperley--Lieb algebra is the main algebraic result of this paper.
4
4

4

It is worth mentioning that a similar Schur–Weyl duality theorem but with finite-dimensional spin-
j
representations of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
at the boundary has been recently proven by Daugherty and Ram
[
17
, Theorem 5.1]
(see also
[
18
]
).

Using this theorem, we can interpret the restriction of
H_{2b}
to
\mathcal{H}_{M}
as a representation of
\mathbf{H}
and thus identify it with
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
satisfying (
1.3
) for
M\geq N
and with an irreducible subblock of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
satisfying (
1.3
) for
0\leq M\leq N-1
(Corollary
2.4
). By a simple algebraic transformation, we are also able to reach the remaining block of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
for
0\leq M\leq N-1
as well as negative values of
M
, thus realising any
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
satisfying (
1.3
) as some subsector of
H_{2b}
(Corollary
2.5
). In other words, the spectral problem of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
for all values of the parameters subject to the constraint (
1.3
) is equivalent to diagonalising
H_{2b}
. But this is a simpler task, since
H_{2b}
, as a representation of
\mathbf{H}
, is integrable
[
24
]
, and, as an operator acting on
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
, is
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant and so has a suitable highest-weight reference eigenvector to implement ABA. This formalism also gives an algebraic interpretation of the Nepomechie condition: it is just a direct consequence of the fusion rules for the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules entering the construction of
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
, in particular Verma modules, which restrict the possible values of the
Y
parameter of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
to the discrete set
\{Y_{M},M\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

It is worth mentioning that the idea to use the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra to derive the BAE for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
and to understand the Nepomechie condition from an algebraic point of view was previously explored in
[
20
]
. However, the lack of a suitable
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant representation in that paper makes it necessary to use coordinate Bethe ansatz in a given basis and keeps the relevant underlying algebra hidden.

The paper is divided into two parts. The first (Section
2
), purely algebraic, introduces the objects and states the theorems we need to make a precise connection between
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
and
H_{2b}
. The second (Section
3
) is devoted to the diagonalisation of
H_{2b}
, first by implementing the ABA procedure for the simpler one-boundary Hamiltonian
H_{b}
(Section
3.1
) and then by extending it to the two-boundary Hamiltonian
H_{2b}
(Section
3.2
). We also discuss the completeness of the BAE in both cases. The main text is supplemented by three technical appendices, the first (Appendix
A
) containing the proof of Theorem
2.3
, the second (Appendix
B
) carrying out ABA for the most general integrable
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant highest-weight spin chain and the third (Appendix
C
) discussing an alternative form of BAE for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
also appearing in the literature.

Notations

•

N
: length of bulk of spin chains.

•

\sigma^{x}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
,
\sigma^{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-{\rm i}\\
{\rm i}&\hphantom{-}0\end{pmatrix}
,
\sigma^{z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\hphantom{-}0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
: Pauli matrices.

•

\sigma^{+}=\dfrac{1}{2}(\sigma^{x}+{\rm i}\sigma^{y})=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
,
\sigma^{-}=\dfrac{1}{2}(\sigma^{x}-{\rm i}\sigma^{y})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
: raising and lowering matrices.

•

\mathcal{H}:=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
: Hilbert space of the open XXZ spin chain of length
N
.

•

H_{{\rm n.d.}}
: open XXZ Hamiltonian with non-diagonal boundary terms.

•

H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
:
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
under the Nepomechie constraint (
1.3
) for
M\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•

\mathsf{TL}_{\delta,N}
: Temperley–Lieb (TL) algebra on
N
sites with loop weight
\delta
.

•

e_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq N-1
: generators of the Temperley–Lieb algebra or their spin-chain representatives.

•

\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y,N}
: blob algebra on
N
sites with loop weight
\delta
and blob weight
y
.

•

b
,
\bar{b}:=1-b
: blob/anti-blob generator or its spin-chain representative.

•

2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
: two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra on
N
sites with loop weight
\delta
, left/right blob weights
y_{l/r}
and two-blob weight
Y
.

•

b_{l/r}
,
\bar{b}_{l/r}:=1-b_{l/r}
: left/right blob/anti-blob generators (
b_{l}:=b
) or their spin-chain representatives.

•

\mathcal{W}_{j}^{bb}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b\bar{b}}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}b}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
,
1\leq j\leq N/2
, and
\mathcal{W}_{0}
: standard
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
-modules.

•

\mathbf{H}:=-\mu_{l}b_{l}-\mu_{r}b_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}\in 2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
: abstract two-boundary Temperley–Lieb Hamiltonian.

•

2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
: universal two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra on
N
sites with loop weight
\delta
and left/right blob weights
y_{l/r}
and central element
\mathsf{Y}
.

•

\mathfrak{q}={\rm e}^{h}
: deformation parameter of the XXZ spin chain.

•

[x]_{\mathfrak{q}}:=\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x}-\mathfrak{q}^{-x}}{\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}
:
\mathfrak{q}
-deformed numbers.

•

\{x\}:=\mathfrak{q}^{x}-\mathfrak{q}^{-x}
.

•

U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
: quantum group, a
\mathfrak{q}
-deformation of
\mathsf{SU}(2)
.

•

\mathsf{E}
,
\mathsf{F}
,
\mathsf{K}
,
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
: generators of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

•

\mathsf{C}
: Casimir element of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

•

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
: infinite-dimensional Verma module of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
of highest-weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha-1}
.

•

H_{{\rm XXZ}}
:
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant open XXZ Hamiltonian.

•

\mathcal{H}_{b}:=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
: Hilbert space of the one-boundary spin chain of length
N
.

•

H_{b}:=-\mu b+H_{{\rm XXZ}}
:
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant one-boundary Hamiltonian with coupling
\mu
.

•

\mathcal{H}_{2b}:=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
: Hilbert space of the two-boundary spin chain of length
N
.

•

H_{2b}:=-\mu_{l}b_{l}+H_{{\rm XXZ}}-\mu_{r}b_{r}
:
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant two-boundary Hamiltonian with couplings
\mu_{l}
,
\mu_{r}
.

•

\mathcal{H}_{M}
,
M\geq 0
: subspaces of highest-weight vectors of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}+N-2M-2}
of
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
.

2
Algebraic setting

In this section, we present the necessary algebraic tools:

•

the relevant lattice algebras, namely the Temperley–Lieb (TL) algebra, the Blob algebra and the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra and their representations (Section
2.1
),

•

the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
quantum group and its representations (Section
2.2
),

•

U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant spin chains with Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{H}_{b}
,
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
and corresponding Hamiltonians
H_{{\rm XXZ}}
,
H_{b}
,
H_{2b}
(Section
2.3
),

•

Schur–Weyl duality between the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and lattice algebra actions on these spin chains (Section
2.3
).

Most of the formalism and results were introduced in
[
14
]
and we will often refer to this paper for additional details. To simplify the exposition we will always assume that
N
is strictly positive and even, but our construction can be extended to the odd
N
case too.

2.1
Lattice algebras

The TL algebra
[
59
]
, denoted
\mathsf{TL}_{\delta,N}
, is defined by generators
(e_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N-1}
and relations

e_{i}^{2}=\delta e_{i},\qquad e_{i}e_{i\pm 1}e_{i}=e_{i},\qquad[e_{i},e_{j}]=0\quad\forall|i-j|\geq 2,

(2.1)

with
\delta\in\mathbb{C}
some parameter. If we set

e_{i}=
.

…
.

…
.

these relations are neatly expressed by the graphical rules

e_{i}^{2}=

…
.
.
.

…
.
.
.

…
.

=
\delta
.
.

.
…

The parameter
\delta
is then interpreted as the weight of a closed loop.

The TL can be shown to be finite-dimensional and all its irreducible representations, called standard modules, have been classified. They are indexed by an integer
0\leq j\leq N/2
interpreted as half the number of through lines propagating in a TL diagram. Concretely, the standard module
\mathcal{W}_{j}
has a basis of so-called link states which are half-diagrams containing exactly
2j
through lines. For example, for
N=4
, these are given by

By some standard combinatorial arguments one can show that

\dim\mathcal{W}_{j}={N\choose\frac{N}{2}-j}-{N\choose\frac{N}{2}-j-1}.

(2.2)

Let us now define some boundary extensions of the TL algebra. The simplest one is the blob algebra introduced in
[
47
]
and denoted
\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y,N}
. It has an additional generator
b
called the blob satisfying

b^{2}=b,\qquad e_{1}be_{1}=ye_{1},\qquad[b,e_{i}]=0\quad\text{for}\quad 2\leq i\leq N-1,

(2.3)

with
y\in\mathbb{C}
some parameter. Graphically,
b
is represented by

b=
.
.
{{{\bullet}}}
.
.
…
.
.
.
.

and the rules (
2.3
) mean that

(2.5)

which justifies the anti-blob terminology.

The blob algebra is also finite-dimensional and the classification of its standard modules is very similar to the analogous construction in the TL algebra. They are also indexed by
0\leq j\leq N/2
and constructed using link states with
2j
through lines but now we also have to decorate all the cups and through lines which can touch the left boundary by blobs and anti-blobs. Since only the leftmost through line can touch it, we have two types of standard modules:
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b}
where the leftmost through line carries a blob and
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}}
where it carries an anti-blob. For
j=0
, there are no through lines so we just have
\mathcal{W}_{0}
.
5
5

5

We will often slightly abuse notation and denote
\mathcal{W}_{0}
the module with no through lines irrespectively of the lattice algebra we consider.

The action of the blob algebra on
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}}
, with
1\leq j\leq N/2
, and
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is defined in the same way as for the TL algebra with the additional diagrammatical rules (
2.4
) and (
2.5
). Moreover, one can show
[
38
,
47
]
that

\dim\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b}=\dim\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}}={N\choose N/2+j},\qquad\dim\mathcal{W}_{0}={N\choose N/2}.

(2.6)

We can further extend the blob algebra by working with two blobs, one on the left, denoted
b_{l}:=b
, with weight
y_{l}:=y
and one on the right, denoted
b_{r}
and represented by

b_{r}=
.
.
\blacksquare
.
.
…
.
.
.
.

and satisfying an analogue of (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) but on the right

\displaystyle b_{r}^{2}=b_{r},\qquad e_{N-1}b_{r}e_{N-1}=y_{r}e_{N-1},

\displaystyle[b_{l},b_{r}]=0,\qquad[b_{r},e_{i}]=0\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq N-2,

(2.7)

with a weight
y_{r}
for a loop carrying the right blob
\blacksquare
. One of course also has a right anti-blob
\bar{b}_{r}=1-b_{r}
represented by
\square
and satisfying (
2.7
) with weight
\delta-y_{r}
together with
b_{r}\bar{b}_{r}=\bar{b}_{r}b_{r}=0
. This is not sufficient from a diagrammatical point of view however as we also need to assign some weight
Y\in\mathbb{C}
to a closed loop carrying both the left and the right blob. Formally, this non-local relation is given by

\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{N/2}e_{2i-1}\Bigg)b_{l}\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{N/2-1}e_{2i}\Bigg)b_{r}\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{N/2}e_{2i-1}\Bigg)=Y\prod_{i=1}^{N/2}e_{2i-1}.

(2.8)

The generators
(e_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N-1}
and
b_{l/r}
with relations (
2.1
), (
2.3
), (
2.7
) and (
2.8
), then define a finite-dimensional algebra called the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra denoted
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
.

The classification of standard modules of the two-boundary TL algebra is a natural generalisation of the blob algebra case
[
19
,
27
]
. Namely, for
1\leq j\leq N
we have four types of modules, namely
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{bb}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}b}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b\bar{b}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
, with
2j
through lines depending on whether the leftmost/rightmost line carries a blob/anti-blob, as well as a single module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
with no through lines, all of these being constructed from link states decorated by left/right blob/anti-blob in all allowed ways. The action of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
on these representations is again given by the defining diagrammatical rules of the algebra. One can show
[
19
,
27
]
that

\dim\mathcal{W}_{j}^{bb}=\dim\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}b}=\dim\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b\bar{b}}=\dim\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}=\sum_{k=j}^{N/2}{N\choose N/2+k},\qquad\dim\mathcal{W}_{0}=2^{N}.

(2.9)

\mathcal{W}_{0}
is called the vacuum module. It is generically irreducible but can become reducible but indecomposable for certain values of the parameters
[
19
]
which will be important for us later on (see Section
2.3
and Appendix
A
). It is also worth mentioning that since a closed loop touching both boundaries can be formed only if there are no through lines,
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is the only standard module which actually depends on the value of
Y
.

The main interest of this whole formalism for the open XXZ spin chain with non-diagonal boundary terms is the following. Let us set

\displaystyle e_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}\big(\sigma^{x}_{i}\sigma^{x}_{i+1}+\sigma^{y}_{i}\sigma^{y}_{i+1}+\cosh(h)\big(\sigma^{z}_{i}\sigma^{z}_{i+1}-1\big)+\sinh(h)\big(\sigma_{i+1}^{z}-\sigma_{i}^{z}\big)\big),

\displaystyle b_{l}=\frac{1}{2\sinh(h\alpha_{l})}\big({\rm i}{\rm e}^{h\theta_{l}}\sigma_{1}^{+}+{\rm i}{\rm e}^{-h\theta_{l}}\sigma_{1}^{-}+\cosh(h\alpha_l)\sigma_{1}^{z}\big)+\frac{1}{2},

\displaystyle b_{r}=-\frac{1}{2\sinh(h\alpha_{r})}\big({\rm i}{\rm e}^{h\theta_{r}}\sigma_{N}^{+}+{\rm i}{\rm e}^{-h\theta_{r}}\sigma_{N}^{-}+\cosh(h\alpha_r)\sigma_{N}^{z}\big)+\frac{1}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{l/r}=\frac{\sinh(h)\sinh\big(h\alpha_{l/r}\big)}{\sinh(\zeta_{l/r}-\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2})\sinh(\zeta_{l/r}+\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2})}

(2.10)

with some new parameters
\alpha_{l/r}
and
\zeta_{l/r}
. Then, up to an irrelevant additive constant, we have

H_{{\rm n.d.}}=-\mu_{l}b_{l}-\mu_{r}b_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}

with
6
6

6

Other choices are possible but this will not affect the end result.

\displaystyle h\delta_{l}=\frac{h\alpha_{l}}{2}-\zeta_{l},\qquad

\displaystyle h\kappa_{l}=\frac{h\alpha_{l}}{2}+\zeta_{l}+\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2},

\displaystyle h\delta_{r}=\frac{h\alpha_{r}}{2}-\zeta_{r},\qquad

\displaystyle h\kappa_{r}=\frac{h\alpha_{r}}{2}+\zeta_{r}-\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2},

(2.11)

and the following result holds.

Theorem 2.1

(J. de Gier, A. Nichols
[
19
]
)
.

The
e_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq N-1
, and
b_{l/r}
from (
2.10
) satisfy the relations of the two-boundary TL algebra with weights

\displaystyle\delta=2\cosh(h),\qquad y_{l/r}=\frac{\sinh\big(h(\alpha_{l/r}+1)\big)}{\sinh\big(h\alpha_{l/r}\big)},

\displaystyle Y=\dfrac{\sinh(h\frac{\alpha_l+\alpha_r+1\pm\Theta}{2})\sinh(h\frac{\alpha_l+\alpha_r+1\mp\Theta}{2})}{\sinh(h\alpha_{l})\sinh(h\alpha_{r})},

(2.12)

where
\Theta:=\theta_{l}-\theta_{r}
(
1.2
), and thus define a
2^{N}
-dimensional representation of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
on
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
. Moreover, this representation is isomorphic to the vacuum module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
.

Using this theorem, we can identify
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
with
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and interpret
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
as an abstract element of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}

\mathbf{H}:=-\mu_{l}b_{l}-\mu_{r}b_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}

(2.13)

evaluated in the vacuum representation
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, that is
H_{{\rm n.d.}}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})
where
\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\colon 2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{W}_{0})
is the representation map of
\mathcal{W}_{0}
. This will be an essential ingredient of our construction. In what follows we will always tacitly make the identification
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}\cong\mathcal{W}_{0}
. To the best of our knowledge, there is no simple way to construct this isomorphism explicitly.

Note also that the weights (
2.12
) do not depend on the sign of
\Theta
, so the spectrum
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
is invariant under the transformation
\Theta\leftrightarrow-\Theta
. In particular, the
+\Theta
and
-\Theta
choices in the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
) are equivalent. When convenient, we will write
\pm\Theta
instead of
\Theta
.

2.2
The
\boldsymbol{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
quantum group

Let us now introduce the second main ingredient: the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
quantum group.

The algebra
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}

[
25
,
40
]
(see also
[
12
, Chapter 6.4]
and
[
43
, Chapters VI and VII]
) is defined by generators
\mathsf{E}
,
\mathsf{F}
,
\mathsf{K}
and
\mathsf{K}^{-1}
and relations

\mathsf{K}\mathsf{E}\mathsf{K}^{-1}=\mathfrak{q}^{2}\mathsf{E},\qquad\mathsf{K}\mathsf{F}\mathsf{K}^{-1}=\mathfrak{q}^{-2}\mathsf{F},\qquad[\mathsf{E},\mathsf{F}]=\frac{\mathsf{K}-\mathsf{K}^{-1}}{\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}},\qquad\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}^{-1}=\mathsf{K}^{-1}\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{1}.

(2.14)

It is a
\mathfrak{q}
-deformation of the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, in the sense that we recover the commutation relations of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
triple
(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{F},\mathsf{H})
in the limit
\mathfrak{q}\to 1
with
\mathsf{K}=\mathfrak{q}^{\mathsf{H}}
. It is important for defining the action on tensor products of representations that this algebra admits the coproduct

\Delta(\mathsf{E})=\mathsf{1}\otimes\mathsf{E}+\mathsf{E}\otimes\mathsf{K},\qquad\Delta(\mathsf{F})=\mathsf{K}^{-1}\otimes\mathsf{F}+\mathsf{F}\otimes\mathsf{1},\qquad\Delta\big(\mathsf{K}^{\pm 1}\big)=\mathsf{K}^{\pm 1}\otimes\mathsf{K}^{\pm 1}\ .

(2.15)

As
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
,
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
admits (
2j+1
)-dimensional spin-
j
representations for all
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
. For our purposes we will need the fundamental spin-
\frac{1}{2}
representation
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, where the action of the generators is given by

\mathsf{E}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=\sigma^{+},\qquad\mathsf{F}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=\sigma^{-},\qquad\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{\pm 1}=\mathfrak{q}^{\pm\sigma^{z}}.

(2.16)

Let us also introduce the Verma modules
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}

[
43
, Chapter VI.3]
that we shall need to define our modified boundary conditions. For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, they are given in a basis
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}:=\bigoplus_{0\leq n}\mathbb{C}\ket{n}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}\ket{n}=[n]_{\mathfrak{q}}[\alpha-n]_{\mathfrak{q}}\ket{n-1},\qquad\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}\ket{n}=\ket{n+1},\qquad\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}^{\pm 1}\ket{n}=\mathfrak{q}^{\pm(\alpha-1-2n)}\ket{n}

(2.17)

for all
n\geq 0
, with
\ket{-1}=0
, and where

[x]_{\mathfrak{q}}:=\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x}-\mathfrak{q}^{-x}}{\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}=\frac{\{x\}}{\{1\}},\qquad\{x\}:=\mathfrak{q}^{x}-\mathfrak{q}^{-x}.

The basis vectors
\ket{n}
diagonalise
\mathsf{K}
and their
\mathsf{K}
-eigenvalue
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha-1-2n}
is called the weight. The vector
\ket{0}
is annihilated by the raising operator
\mathsf{E}
and is thus called the highest-weight vector. Note that its weight is
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha-1}
with the
-1
shift of
\alpha
introduced for later convenience. When
\mathfrak{q}
is not a root of unity,
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
is irreducible if and only if
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}\neq\pm\mathfrak{q}^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
7
7

7

If
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}\in\pm\mathfrak{q}^{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
contains a unique non-trivial stable subspace but is indecomposable.
If that is the case,
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
is also unique, meaning that any
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-module generated from a highest-weight vector of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha-1}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
. Finally, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}\neq\pm 1
we have the fusion rule
8
8

8

If
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}=\pm 1
, the two factors
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha+1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha-1}
get “glued” into a single indecomposable representation known as a tilting module.

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong\mathcal{V}_{\alpha+1}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha-1}.

(2.18)

The above definitions have to be slightly adapted if
\mathfrak{q}
is a root of unity. This case was thoroughly treated in
[
14
]
and presents no major complications. To keep the exposition simple, from now on we will always assume
\mathfrak{q}
to be generic (not a root of unity) unless otherwise stated. We refer to
[
14
]
for further details.

2.3
\boldsymbol{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
-invariant spin chains and Schur–Weyl duality

2.3.1
The bulk spin chain

Applying the coproduct (
2.15
)
N-1
times (recall that the coproduct is coassociative, and so the result does not depend on the order of its application) to the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
representation (
2.16
) we obtain a well-defined action of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
on
\mathcal{H}:=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
. Now if we set

\displaystyle e_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\sigma^{x}_{i}\sigma^{x}_{i+1}+\sigma^{y}_{i}\sigma^{y}_{i+1}+\frac{\mathfrak{q}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}{2}\big(\sigma^{z}_{i}\sigma^{z}_{i+1}-1\big)\bigg)-\frac{\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}{4}\big(\sigma_{i+1}^{z}-\sigma_{i}^{z}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{e_i}{}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}0&0\\
0&\hphantom{-}\mathfrak{q}&-1&0\\
0&-1&\hphantom{-}\mathfrak{q}^{-1}&0\\
0&\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}0&0\end{pmatrix}

(2.19)

as in (
2.10
), with
\mathfrak{q}={\rm e}^{h}
, it turns out that the
e_{i}
commute with this
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
action
[
56
]
. This implies that the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{{\rm XXZ}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\bigg(\sigma^{x}_{i}\sigma^{x}_{i+1}+\sigma^{y}_{i}\sigma^{y}_{i+1}+\frac{\mathfrak{q}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}{2}\sigma^{z}_{i}\sigma^{z}_{i+1}\bigg)+\frac{\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}{4}\big(\sigma_{N}^{z}-\sigma_{1}^{z}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{q}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}}{2}(N-1)-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}

(2.20)

(which is just the first line of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
from (
1.1
), i.e., without the two boundary terms) is
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant. More generally, the whole
\mathsf{TL}_{\delta,N}
-action on
\mathcal{H}
generated by the
e_{i}
commutes with
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. Actually, not only they commute, but they are even mutual maximal centralisers and
\mathcal{H}
decomposes as a
(\mathsf{TL}_{\delta,N},U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-bimodule
[
34
,
41
,
48
,
49
]

\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{N/2}\mathcal{W}_{j}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1},

(2.21)

where
\mathcal{W}_{j}
are the standard
\mathsf{TL}_{\delta,N}
-modules introduced above and
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}
are spin-
j
representations of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. This result is known as (quantum) Schur–Weyl duality. This result is essential, as it reduces the study of
H_{{\rm XXZ}}
on
\mathcal{H}:=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
to its restriction on standard
\mathsf{TL}_{\delta,N}
-modules.

Unfortunately, we cannot use this method for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
because the boundary terms (
2.10
)

\displaystyle b_{l}=\frac{1}{\{\alpha_{l}\}}\left({\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{\theta_{l}}\sigma_{1}^{+}+{\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{-\theta_{l}}\sigma_{1}^{-}+\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}}+\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha_{l}}}{2}\sigma_{1}^{z}\right)+\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{\{\alpha_{l}\}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}}&{\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{\theta_{l}}\\
{\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{-\theta_{l}}&-\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha_{l}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle b_{r}=-\frac{1}{\{\alpha_{r}\}}\left({\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{\theta_{r}}\sigma_{N}^{+}+{\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{-\theta_{r}}\sigma_{N}^{-}+\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{r}}+\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha_{r}}}{2}\sigma_{N}^{z}\right)+\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{\{\alpha_{r}\}}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha_{r}}&-{\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{\theta_{r}}\\
-{\rm i}\mathfrak{q}^{-\theta_{r}}&\hphantom{-}\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{r}}\end{pmatrix}

break the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
symmetry. This is why we are going to build a different Hamiltonian (
2.29
) which does preserve the quantum group symmetry and then show that it can be related back to
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
through the two-boundary TL algebra.

2.3.2
The one-boundary system

Following
[
14
]
, let us first introduce the one-boundary Hamiltonian. It is constructed by tensoring the usual spin chain
\mathcal{H}:=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
with the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
and adding a new
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant boundary term acting on the two leftmost sites,
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
, of the new Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}_{b}:=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}.

Because of the fusion rule (
2.18
) the most general such term can only be a linear combination of projectors
b_{\pm}
on the direct summands
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha\pm 1}
, which are given by

b_{\pm}=\frac{\pm 1}{\{\alpha\}}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathsf{K}^{-1}+\mathfrak{q}^{\pm\alpha}&\{1\}\mathsf{F}\\
\mathfrak{q}\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{-1}\mathsf{E}&\mathfrak{q}\mathsf{K}^{-1}-\mathfrak{q}^{\mp\alpha}\end{pmatrix}.

(2.22)

In the expression above,
b_{\pm}
are operators acting on
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
which we have written as
2\times 2
matrices with entries in
\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha})
. Since
b_{+}+b_{-}=1
, it is sufficient, up to irrelevant additive terms in the Hamiltonian, to consider boundary couplings of the form
-\mu b
, with
b:=b_{+}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
a coupling constant. The one-boundary Hamiltonian on
\mathcal{H}_{b}
is then defined as

H_{b}:=-\mu b-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}.

(2.23)

One can also check that
b
satisfies

b^{2}=b,\qquad e_{1}be_{1}=ye_{1},\qquad[b,e_{i}]=0\quad\text{for}\quad 2\leq i\leq N-1

(2.24)

with

y=\frac{[\alpha+1]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{[\alpha]_{\mathfrak{q}}}.

(2.25)

Together with (
2.1
) this means that
\mathcal{H}_{b}
carries a representation of the blob algebra. By construction, the actions of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y,N}
on
\mathcal{H}_{b}
commute with each other. One can actually show
[
14
]
that if
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}\notin\pm\mathfrak{q}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have Schur–Weyl duality, namely
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y,N}
are mutual maximal centralisers and we have the
(\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y,N},U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-bimodule decomposition

\mathcal{H}_{b}=\mathcal{W}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N/2}\big(\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha+2j}\oplus\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha-2j}\big).

(2.26)

2.3.3
The two-boundary system

The two-boundary Hamiltonian is constructed in very much the same way, this time by tensoring the usual spin chain
\mathcal{H}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
with two Verma modules
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
, on the left and on the right respectively
[
14
]
. The most general left boundary coupling is still given by
-\mu_{l}b_{l}
with
b_{l}
the projector on
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+1}
from (
2.22
). On the other hand, the projector on the
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}+1}
summand of
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}\cong\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}+1}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}-1}
is

b_{r}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{q}\mathsf{K}-\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha_{r}}&\mathfrak{q}\{1\}\mathsf{K}\mathsf{F}\\
\{1\}\mathsf{E}&-\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathsf{K}+\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{r}}\end{pmatrix}.

(2.27)

The two-boundary Hamiltonian is then defined on the Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}_{2b}:=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}

(2.28)

as

H_{2b}:=-\mu_{l}b_{l}-\mu_{r}b_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}

(2.29)

with
\mu_{r}\in\mathbb{C}
a coupling constant. Similarly to
b_{l}
, the new generator
b_{r}
satisfies

b_{r}^{2}=b_{r},\qquad e_{N-1}b_{r}e_{N-1}=y_{r}e_{N-1},\qquad[b_{r},e_{i}]=0\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq N-2

(2.30)

with

y_{r}=\frac{[\alpha_{r}+1]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{[\alpha_{r}]_{\mathfrak{q}}}.

(2.31)

To obtain a representation of the two-boundary TL algebra it remains to compute the weight
Y
of a loop carrying both
b_{r}
and
b_{l}
. We can indeed find such a
Y
, but it turns out that in our case it will not be a number but some non-trivial central element
[
14
]
. This is why we need to use a slightly different version of the two-boundary TL algebra, namely the
universal
two-boundary TL algebra
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
. It is defined by the same relations as
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
but now
Y
is treated as an additional generator denoted
\mathsf{Y}
and commuting with all the other generators
e_{i}
and
b_{l/r}
, i.e., it is a central extension of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
. If we want to recover the usual two-boundary TL at some fixed value of
Y\in\mathbb{C}
, we just have to take the quotient
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}/\langle\mathsf{Y}-Y\rangle\cong 2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
. In particular, this implies that any representation of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
for any value of
Y\in\mathbb{C}
is automatically a representation of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
(the converse is not true in general, however). Note also that contrary to the usual two-boundary TL algebra,
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}\supset\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{Y}]
is infinite-dimensional.

Coming back to our spin chain, we showed in
[
14
]
that
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
carries a representation of the universal two-boundary TL algebra
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
. Concretely,
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
admits a Casimir element

\mathsf{C}:=\{1\}^{2}\mathsf{F}\mathsf{E}+\mathfrak{q}\mathsf{K}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathsf{K}^{-1},

(2.32)

which commutes with
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and moreover its action on
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
, denoted
\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}
, commutes with the
e_{i}
and
b_{l/r}
. Then the relation (
2.8
) is satisfied for
9
9

9

This result, shown in
[
14
]
, has also a natural interpretation in terms of the affine Hecke algebra of type
C

[
17
, Section 3.7]
.

\mathsf{Y}=\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}+1}+\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha_{l}-\alpha_{r}-1}-\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}}{\{\alpha_{l}\}\{\alpha_{r}\}}

(2.33)

making
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
a representation of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
. By construction, this action commutes with that of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

Following
[
14
]
, we can restrict the action of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
to a
\mathsf{Y}
-eigenspace to obtain a well-defined action of the usual two-boundary TL algebra
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
for some fixed value of
Y
. Since the Casimir
\mathsf{C}
commutes with
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
it acts as a scalar on any irreducible representation of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, in particular
10
10

10

Just evaluate it on the highest-weight vector:
\mathsf{C}\ket{0}=\{1\}^{2}\mathsf{F}\mathsf{E}\ket{0}+\mathfrak{q}\mathsf{K}\ket{0}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathsf{K}^{-1}\ket{0}=\left(\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}+\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha}\right)\ket{0}
.

\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}=\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}+\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha},

(2.34)

and so this amounts to computing the decomposition of
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
into simple
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules. Using the fusion rule for Verma modules, valid for
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1}\notin\pm\mathfrak{q}^{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
,
11
11

11

As for the fusion rule (
2.18
), if this condition is not satisfied, some of the summands are glued into tilting modules.

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}\cong\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1-2n}

(2.35)

as well as (
2.18
), we obtain the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-decomposition

\mathcal{H}_{2b}=\bigoplus_{M\geq 0}\mathcal{H}_{M}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},

(2.36)

where
\mathcal{H}_{M}
are some multiplicity spaces of dimension

d_{M}:=\dim\mathcal{H}_{M}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{M}{N\choose k}&\text{for}\ 0\leq M\leq N,\\
2^{N}&\text{for}\ M\geq N,\end{cases}

(2.37)

which can be identified with the subspaces of highest-weight vectors of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-2+N-2M}
. By direct computation, one then shows
[
14
]
that restricted to
\mathcal{H}_{M}
,
\mathsf{Y}
acts as the scalar

Y_{M}=\dfrac{\left[M+1-\frac{N}{2}\right]_{\mathfrak{q}}\left[\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-M+\frac{N}{2}\right]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{[\alpha_{l}]_{\mathfrak{q}}[\alpha_{r}]_{\mathfrak{q}}}

(2.38)

and so, for all
M\geq 0
,
\mathcal{H}_{M}
is a representation of the two-boundary TL algebra
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
.

The final question is what are these representations. Let us first recall an important result from
[
19
,
27
]
, valid for generic
\mathfrak{q}
.

Theorem 2.2
.

(i)

For
0\leq M\leq N/2-1
the vacuum module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
is reducible but indecomposable, with a unique irreducible proper
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-submodule isomorphic to
\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}
and an irreducible subquotient
\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}
.

(ii)

For
N/2\leq M\leq N-1
the vacuum module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
is reducible but indecomposable, with a unique irreducible proper
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-submodule isomorphic to
\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
and an irreducible subquotient
\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
.

(iii)

For
M\geq N
the vacuum module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
is irreducible.

Based on this theorem and the dimensions (
2.37
) a conjecture about the nature of the
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-modules
\mathcal{H}_{M}
was made in
[
14
]
. In Appendix
A
, we prove this conjecture, so let us restate it here as a theorem:

Theorem 2.3
.

For
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathfrak{q}^{i\pi\mathbb{Q}}
,
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}},\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{r}}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\{\pm\mathfrak{q}^{\mathbb{Z}}\}
such that
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}}\notin\pm\mathfrak{q}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
N\in 2\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
are mutual centralisers on
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
in (
2.28
), with generators
e_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq N-1
,
b_{l}
,
b_{r}
,
\mathsf{Y}
acting by (
2.19
), (
2.22
), (
2.27
), (
2.33
) respectively, and we have the
(2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni},U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-bimodule decomposition

\mathcal{H}_{2b}=\bigoplus_{M\geq 0}\mathcal{H}_{M}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},

(2.39)

where the
\mathcal{H}_{M}
are irreducible
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-modules given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{M}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}\varsubsetneq\mathcal{W}_{0}\qquad\;\,\text{for}~~0\leq M\leq N/2-1,

(2.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{M}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}\,\,\qquad\text{for}~~N/2\leq M\leq N-1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{M}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{W}_{0}\,\,\ \qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{for}\ N\leq M.

This theorem means, in particular, that even for
0\leq M\leq N-1
,
\mathcal{H}_{M}
is always isomorphic to an irreducible piece of the vacuum module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
, either a stable subspace (
0\leq M\leq N/2-1
) or an irreducible subquotient (
N/2\leq M\leq N-1
) of
\mathcal{W}_{0}
.

Now recall
\mathfrak{q}={\rm e}^{h}
,
\delta=2\cosh(h)=[2]_{\mathfrak{q}}
, and compare (
2.12
) with (
2.25
)–(
2.38
). All the weights coincide, except for
Y
, which however matches in both cases if and only if

\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}\pm\Theta=2M+1-N,

where
\Theta:=\theta_{l}-\theta_{r}
(
1.2
). Recalling the reparametrisation (
2.11
), this is equivalent to

\delta_{l}+\kappa_{l}+\delta_{r}+\kappa_{r}\pm\Theta=2M+1-N,

which is exactly the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
)! Moreover, Theorems
2.1
–
2.3
imply the following. Set

H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}:=H_{{\rm n.d.}}(\delta,y_{l/r},\mu_{l/r},Y_{M})

and for any
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
-module
\mathcal{M}
denote the representation map
\rho_{\mathcal{M}}\colon 2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{M})
. Then

Corollary 2.4
.

(i)

For
0\leq M\leq N/2-1
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}}(\mathbf{H})&*\\
0&\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}}(\mathbf{H})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}}(\mathbf{H})&*\\
0&H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}\end{pmatrix}.

(ii)

For
N/2\leq M\leq N-1
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}}(\mathbf{H})&*\\
0&\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}}(\mathbf{H})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}&*\\
0&\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}}(\mathbf{H})\end{pmatrix}.

(iii)

For
M\geq N
,

H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})=H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}.

In other words, the spectrum of all the open non-diagonal XXZ Hamiltonians with non-diagonal boundary terms for
all
the values of the parameters covered by the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
) with
M\geq N
is contained in the irreducible sectors of a
single
Hamiltonian
H_{2b}
. For
0\leq M\leq N-1
the sectors of
H_{2b}
only contain an irreducible block of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
. The spectral problem of
H_{2b}
will be solved in the next section by algebraic Bethe ansatz.

One may wonder if we can also express the remaining blocks
\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{N\!/2-\!M}^{bb}}\!\!(\mathbf{H})
and
\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N\!/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}}\!\!(\mathbf{H})
in terms of
H_{2b}
to diagonalise
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
completely, even for
0\leq M\leq N-1
. This turns out to be possible via the following trick. Let us introduce the involution

\overline{\,\cdot\,}\colon\ 2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}\to 2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,\delta-y_{l/r},\delta-y_{l}-y_{r}+Y,N},\qquad(e_{i},b_{l/r})\mapsto(e_{i},\bar{b}_{l/r}:=1-b_{l/r}).

(2.41)

From the definitions of the various weights one easily sees that it is an algebra isomorphism and moreover that it exchanges
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{bb}
with
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}

(
and also
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b\bar{b}}
with
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}b}
)
for
1\leq j\leq N/2
, while leaving
\mathcal{W}_{0}
invariant. Let us define

\overline{\mathbf{H}}:=-\mu_{l}\bar{b}_{l}-\mu_{r}\bar{b}_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}\in 2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,\delta-y_{l/r},\delta-y_{l}-y_{r}+Y,N}.

and

\overline{H}_{2b}:=\rho_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{2b}}\big(\overline{\mathbf{H}}\big)=-\mu_{l}-\mu_{r}+H_{2b}(\alpha_{l/r}\to-\alpha_{l/r},\mu_{l/r}\to-\mu_{l/r})

(2.42)

acting on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{2b}:=\mathcal{V}_{-\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{-\alpha_{r}}
. Note that by (
2.25
)–(
2.31
)

y_{l/r}(\alpha_{l/r}\to-\alpha_{l/r})=\frac{[\alpha_{l/r}-1]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{[\alpha_{l/r}]_{\mathfrak{q}}}=\delta-y_{l/r}

and using (
2.38
) one easily checks that

Y_{M}(\alpha_{l/r}\to-\alpha_{l/r})=\dfrac{\left[\frac{N}{2}-M-1\right]_{\mathfrak{q}}\left[\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-\frac{N}{2}+M\right]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{[\alpha_{l}]_{\mathfrak{q}}[\alpha_{r}]_{\mathfrak{q}}}=\delta-y_{l}-y_{r}+Y_{N-M-1},

(2.43)

so by Theorem
2.3
,
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{2b}
decomposes as

\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{2b}=\bigoplus_{M\geq 0}\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{-\alpha_{l}-\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},

where the
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
are irreducible
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,\delta-y_{l/r},\delta-y_{l}-y_{r}+Y_{N-M-1},N}
-modules given by (
2.40
). Pulling back by the algebra isomorphism (
2.41
), we can thus generalise Corollary
2.4
.

Corollary 2.5
.

(i)

For
0\leq M\leq N/2-1
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\big(\overline{\mathbf{H}}\big)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}}\big(\overline{\mathbf{H}}\big)&*\\
0&\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}}(\mathbf{H})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{H}_{2b}|_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{N-M-1}}&*\\
0&H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}\end{pmatrix}.

(ii)

For
N/2\leq M\leq N-1
,

\displaystyle H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\big(\overline{\mathbf{H}}\big)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}}(\mathbf{H})&*\\
0&\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{bb}}\big(\overline{\mathbf{H}}\big)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}&*\\
0&\overline{H}_{2b}|_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{N-M-1}}\end{pmatrix}.

(iii)

For
M\geq N
,

H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}(\mathbf{H})=H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}.

(iv)

For
M\leq-1
,

H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}=\rho_{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\big(\overline{\mathbf{H}}\big)=\overline{H}_{2b}|_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{N-M-1}}.

Note that, because of (
2.43
), the magnon number
M
labelling the sectors
\mathcal{H}_{M}
of
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
is mapped to the dual magnon number
\overline{M}:=N-M-1
in the
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{2b}
spin chain (and vice versa) by the involution (
2.41
). It is this purely algebraic observation that enables us to reach the missing blocks of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
for
0\leq M\leq N-1
as well as negative values of
M
, which correspond to
\overline{M}\geq N
. As we will see, this will be essential to establish a complete set of Bethe ansatz equations for the degenerate cases
0\leq M\leq N-1
.

To summarise, we have reduced the spectral problem of all the
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
,
M\in\mathbb{Z}
, satisfying the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
) to the spectral problem of
H_{2b}
.
12
12

12

Note that
\overline{H}_{2b}
is related to
H_{2b}
by the transformation (
2.42
) so it is sufficient to consider
H_{2b}
only.
This may not seem like a big step, but actually it is:
H_{2b}
is
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant and as such has a natural reference state
\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes N}\otimes\ket{0}
which will enable us to compute its spectrum using standard algebraic Bethe ansatz.

3
Bethe ansatz

We now turn to the computation of the spectrum of
H_{2b}
(
2.29
) using the algebraic boundary Bethe ansatz formalism first developed by Sklyanin
[
58
]
. Here, we no longer assume that
N
is even. As a warm-up, we will first treat the one-boundary Hamiltonian
H_{b}
(
2.23
).

3.1
The one-boundary system

In the basis
\{\ket{\uparrow\uparrow},\ket{\uparrow\downarrow},\ket{\downarrow\uparrow},\ket{\downarrow\downarrow}\}
the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant (affine)
R
-matrix is given by

R(u):=\begin{pmatrix}\sinh(u+h)&0&0&0\\
0&\sinh(u)&\sinh(h){\rm e}^{u}&0\\
0&\sinh(h){\rm e}^{-u}&\sinh(u)&0\\
0&0&0&\sinh(u+h)\end{pmatrix}.

(3.1)

It is easy to check that

\check{R}_{i,i+1}(u):=P_{i,i+1}R_{i,i+1}(u)=\sinh(u+h)-\sinh(u)e_{i},

(3.2)

where
P_{i,i+1}
is the operator permuting the
i
and
(i+1)
-th sites of the spin chain. For all
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
, the
R
-matrix
R(u)
satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation (YBE)

R_{1,2}(u-v)R_{1,3}(u)R_{2,3}(v)=R_{2,3}(v)R_{1,3}(u)R_{1,2}(u-v),

(3.3)

where
R_{i,j}(u)
denotes
R(u)
acting the
i
-th and
j
-th tensor factors of
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 3}
or, equivalently,

\check{R}_{1,2}(u-v)\check{R}_{2,3}(u)\check{R}_{1,2}(v)=\check{R}_{2,3}(v)\check{R}_{1,2}(u)\check{R}_{2,3}(u-v).

Note also that

R_{1,2}(u)R_{2,1}(-u)=\sinh(h+u)\sinh(h-u)\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}},

(3.4)

so
R(u)
is invertible if
u\neq\pm h
.

For boundary Bethe ansatz, one also needs an additional ingredient: a so-called
K
-matrix. It is a
2\times 2
matrix
K(u)
with entries in some (possibly non-commutative) algebra satisfying the boundary Yang–Baxter equation (bYBE)

R_{1,2}(u-v)K_{1}(u)R_{2,1}(u+v)K_{2}(v)=K_{2}(v)R_{1,2}(u+v)K_{1}(u)R_{2,1}(u-v)

(3.5)

or, written differently,

\check{R}_{1,2}(u-v)K_{1}(u)\check{R}_{1,2}(u+v)K_{1}(v)=K_{1}(v)\check{R}_{1,2}(u+v)K_{1}(u)\check{R}_{1,2}(u-v).

(3.6)

For example, because

R_{1,2}(u-v)R_{2,1}(u+v)=R_{1,2}(u+v)R_{2,1}(u-v),

(3.7)

K(u)=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
is a solution of (
3.5
). This can also be seen directly from (
3.2
)–(
3.6
).

Although many other solutions to the bYBE (
3.5
) are known
[
21
,
22
,
24
,
58
]
, it is not always possible to find a
K
-matrix yielding precisely the boundary conditions we want to impose. In our case however, it is possible to use the symmetry of the one-boundary spin chain to find a suitable solution of the bYBE (
3.5
). Actually, there are even two independent constructions: one based on the
\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y,N}
-module structure of the spin chain and the other on the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
symmetry.

The first was described in detail
[
24
]
. Namely, for any
b
satisfying the blob algebra relations (
2.24
) there exists a solution of the bYBE (
3.5
) for
H_{b}
. With our choice of
b
this
K
-matrix reads

\displaystyle K(u)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\mu}{\sinh(h)\sinh(h\alpha)}\left(\sinh(u-h\alpha)\sinh(u)+\sinh(h\alpha)\sinh(2u)b\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}}-\frac{\mu\sinh(u)\cosh(u)}{\sinh(h)\sinh(h\alpha)}\!\begin{pmatrix}-{\rm e}^{-h}\mathsf{K}^{-1}+\frac{{\rm e}^{u}\cosh(h\alpha)}{\cosh(u)}&2\sinh(h)\mathsf{F}\\
2{\rm e}^{h}\sinh(h)\mathsf{K}^{-1}\mathsf{E}&{\rm e}^{h}\mathsf{K}^{-1}-\frac{{\rm e}^{-u}\cosh(h\alpha)}{\cosh(u)}\end{pmatrix},\!\!\!

(3.8)

where again we have written it as a
2\times 2
matrix with entries in
\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha})
. The second construction based on the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
symmetry of the spin chain will be used for the two-boundary case where it is most convenient. For the time being, let us work with the
K
-matrix (
3.8
).

Let us define the transfer matrix
13
13

13

The
\frac{h}{2}
shift is introduced to make the final result neater.

t_{b}(u):=\operatorname{qtr}_{0}{T(u-h/2)K(u-h/2)\hat{T}(u-h/2)},

(3.9)

where
K
is treated as a
2\times 2
matrix with entries in
\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha})
acting on the auxiliary
\mathbb{C}^{2}
space (with index
0
),

\displaystyle T(u):=R_{0,N}(u)\cdots R_{0,1}(u),\qquad\hat{T}(u):=R_{1,0}(u)\cdots R_{N,0}(u)

(3.10)

and
\operatorname{qtr}_{0}(-):=\tr_{0}\bigl(\mathfrak{q}^{\sigma^{z}_{0}}-\bigr)
denotes the partial quantum trace over the auxiliary space. This is the natural trace for
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant objects and it ensures that
t_{b}(u)
commutes with
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
as it should (see
[
24
]
for more details
14
14

14

The formalism in this paper is a bit more general with
J
corresponding to our
\mathfrak{q}^{\sigma^{z}_{0}}
.
).

Now by (
3.3
) and (
3.5
)

[t_{b}(u),t_{b}(v)]=0

and, moreover,
[
58
]

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\bigg\rvert_{u=\frac{h}{2}}t_{b}(u)={}

\displaystyle 2\sinh^{2N-1}(h)\tr_{0}\big(\mathfrak{q}^{\sigma^{z}}\big)\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\check{R}^{\prime}_{i,i+1}(0)

\displaystyle{}+2\sinh^{2N-1}(h)\operatorname{qtr}_{0}\big(\check{R}^{\prime}_{N,0}(0)\big)+\sinh^{2N}(h)\tr_{0}\big(\mathfrak{q}^{\sigma^{z}}\big)P_{0,1}K^{\prime}(0)P_{0,1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 4\sinh^{2N}(h)\coth(h)\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\cosh(h)-e_{i}

\displaystyle{}+4\sinh^{2N}(h)\coth(h)\left(\cosh(h)-\frac{1}{2\cosh(h)}\right)

\displaystyle{}+4\sinh^{2N}(h)\coth(h)\left(-\mu b+\frac{\mu}{2}\right),

so

H_{b}=-\mu b-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}=\frac{\tanh(h)}{4\sinh^{2N}(h)}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\bigg\rvert_{u=\frac{h}{2}}t_{b}(u)-N\cosh(h)+\frac{1}{2\cosh(h)}-\frac{\mu}{2}.

(3.11)

Therefore, we are reduced to computing the spectrum of
t_{b}(u)
.

Let us now define the monodromy

\mathcal{T}(u):=T(u-h/2)K(u-h/2)\hat{T}(u-h/2)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(u)&\mathcal{B}(u)\\
\mathcal{C}(u)&\mathcal{D}(u)\end{pmatrix},

where the coefficients of this auxiliary space
2\times 2
matrix are in
\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{H}_{b})
. Repeatedly applying the YBE (
3.3
), one finds that
T(u)
satisfies the RTT relation

R_{0,\bar{0}}(u-v)T_{0}(u)T_{\bar{0}}(v)=T_{\bar{0}}(v)T_{0}(u)R_{0,\bar{0}}(u-v)

(3.12)

involving two different auxiliary
\mathbb{C}^{2}
spaces with index
0
and
\bar{0}
. Note also (
3.4
) implies that
\hat{T}(u)\propto T(-u)^{-1}
. By a general result
[
58
, Proposition 2]
, for any
T(u)
satisfying (
3.12
) and any solution
K(u)
of the bYBE (
3.5
), the product
T(u)K(u)T(-u)^{-1}
is also a solution of (
3.5
). Therefore,
\mathcal{T}(u+h/2)
satisfies (
3.5
)
15
15

15

This statement can also be proved by induction on
N
using only the YBE (
3.3
) and (
3.7
).
, that is,

\displaystyle R_{1,2}(u-v)\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(u)&\mathcal{B}(u)\\
\mathcal{C}(u)&\mathcal{D}(u)\end{pmatrix}_{1}R_{2,1}(u+v-h)\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(v)&\mathcal{B}(v)\\
\mathcal{C}(v)&\mathcal{D}(v)\end{pmatrix}_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(v)&\mathcal{B}(v)\\
\mathcal{C}(v)&\mathcal{D}(v)\end{pmatrix}_{2}R_{1,2}(u+v-h)\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(u)&\mathcal{B}(u)\\
\mathcal{C}(u)&\mathcal{D}(u)\end{pmatrix}_{1}R_{2,1}(u-v).

(3.13)

From the explicit expression of
R(u)
(
3.1
), we can then derive the relevant commutation relations between
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
at different values of the spectral parameter. Doing so, we obtain

[\mathcal{B}(u),\mathcal{B}(v)]=0

(3.14)

for all
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
and, moreover,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\mathcal{B}(v)=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sinh(u-v-h)\sinh(u+v-h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(v)\mathcal{A}(u)

\displaystyle+\frac{{\rm e}^{u-v}\sinh(h)\sinh(u+v-h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(u)\mathcal{A}(v)-\frac{{\rm e}^{u+v-h}\sinh(h)}{\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(u)\mathcal{D}(v),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(u)\mathcal{B}(v)=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sinh(u-v+h)\sinh(u+v+h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(v)\mathcal{D}(u)

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm e}^{v-u}\sinh(h)\sinh(u+v+h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(u)\mathcal{D}(v)

\displaystyle+\frac{{\rm e}^{-u-v+h}\sinh(h)\sinh(u-v+2h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(u)\mathcal{A}(v)

\displaystyle-\frac{2{\rm e}^{-2u+h}\sinh^{2}(h)\cosh(h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(v)\mathcal{A}(u).

Introducing

\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)=\frac{{\rm e}^{-h}\sinh(2u)}{\sinh(2u-h)}\mathcal{D}(u)-\frac{{\rm e}^{-2u}\sinh(h)}{\sinh(2u-h)}\mathcal{A}(u),

(3.15)

these equations become

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\mathcal{B}(v)=\frac{\sinh(u-v-h)\sinh(u+v-h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(v)\mathcal{A}(u)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{A}(u)\mathcal{B}(v) =}{}+f_{1}(u,v)\mathcal{B}(u)\mathcal{A}(v)+f_{2}(u,v)\mathcal{B}(u)\bar{\mathcal{D}}(v),

(3.16)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\mathcal{B}(v)=\frac{\sinh(u-v+h)\sinh(u+v+h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(u+v)}\mathcal{B}(v)\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\mathcal{B}(v) =}{}+g_{1}(u,v)\mathcal{B}(u)\bar{\mathcal{D}}(v)+g_{2}(u,v)\mathcal{B}(u)\mathcal{A}(v),

(3.17)

where

f_{1}(u,v)=\frac{{\rm e}^{u-v}\sinh(h)\sinh(2v-h)}{\sinh(u-v)\sinh(2v)},\qquad f_{2}(u,v)=-\frac{{\rm e}^{u+v}\sinh(h)\sinh(2v-h)}{\sinh(u+v)\sinh(2v)}

(3.18)

and

g_{1}(u,v)=\frac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u-h)}f_{1}(u,v),\qquad g_{2}(u,v)=\frac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u-h)}f_{2}(u,v).

(3.19)

We will actually never need the explicit expressions of
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
(
3.18
) but only the relations (
3.19
). The transfer matrix reads

t_{b}(u)=\operatorname{qtr}_{0}\mathcal{T}(u)={\rm e}^{h}\mathcal{A}(u)+{\rm e}^{-h}\mathcal{D}(u)=\frac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u)}\mathcal{A}(u)+\frac{\sinh(2u-h)}{\sinh(2u)}\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u).

(3.20)

Let us now look for eigenvectors of
t_{b}(u)
of the form

\ket{\{v_m\}}=\mathcal{B}(v_{1})\cdots\mathcal{B}(v_{M})\ket{\Uparrow},

(3.21)

where

\ket{\Uparrow}:=\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes N}

is our reference state (recall that
\ket{0}
is the highest-weight vector of the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
(
2.17
)),
M\geq 0
is the magnon number and
\{v_{m}\}_{1\leq m\leq M}
are some complex numbers that we want to determine. Note that because of (
3.14
), the order of the
v_{m}
is irrelevant. Also
\mathcal{B}(u)
decreases the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-weight by
\mathfrak{q}^{-2}
so

\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{b}}\ket{\{v_m\}}=\mathfrak{q}^{-2M}\mathcal{B}(v_{1})\cdots\mathcal{B}(v_{M})\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{b}}\ket{\Uparrow}=\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha-1+N-2M}\ket{\{v_m\}}

(3.22)

meaning that
\ket{\{v_m\}}
has weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha-1+N-2M}
.

The first step is to compute the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}(u)
and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)
when acting on
\ket{\Uparrow}
. Rewrite (
3.8
)–(
3.10
)

\displaystyle T(u-h/2)=\begin{pmatrix}A(u)&\tilde{B}(u)\\
C(u)&D(u)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\hat{T}(u-h/2)=\begin{pmatrix}A(u)&B(u)\\
\tilde{C}(u)&D(u)\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle K(u-h/2)=\begin{pmatrix}a(u)&b(u)\\
c(u)&d(u)\end{pmatrix}

(3.23)

as
2\times 2
matrices acting on the auxiliary space with coefficients in
\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{H}_{b})
such that

\mathcal{T}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(u)&\mathcal{B}(u)\\
\mathcal{C}(u)&\mathcal{D}(u)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A(u)&\tilde{B}(u)\\
C(u)&D(u)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a(u)&b(u)\\
c(u)&d(u)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A(u)&B(u)\\
\tilde{C}(u)&D(u)\end{pmatrix}.

(3.24)

Knowing that
C(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=\tilde{C}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=a(u)A(u)^{2}\ket{\Uparrow},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=(a(u)C(u)B(u)+d(u)D(u)^{2})\ket{\Uparrow}.

(3.25)

Introducing a basis
\{\ket{\uparrow_0},\ket{\downarrow_0}\}
of the auxiliary space and the matrix entries
r_{ij}:=R(u-h/2)_{ij}
,
1\leq i,j\leq 4
, we obtain

\displaystyle A(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=\bra{\uparrow_0}R_{0,N}(u-h/2)\cdots R_{0,1}(u-h/2)\ket{\uparrow_0}\otimes\ket{\Uparrow}=r_{11}^{N}\ket{\Uparrow}=\sinh(u+h/2)^{N}\ket{\Uparrow},

\displaystyle D(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=\bra{\downarrow_0}R_{0,N}(u-h/2)\cdots R_{0,1}(u-h/2)\ket{\downarrow_0}\otimes\ket{\Uparrow}=r_{33}^{N}\ket{\Uparrow}=\sinh(u-h/2)^{N}\ket{\Uparrow}

and

\displaystyle B(u)\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle=\bra{\uparrow_0}R_{1,0}(u-h/2)\cdots R_{N,0}(u-h/2)\ket{\downarrow_0}\otimes\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle=\bra{\uparrow_0}R_{1,0}(u-h/2)\cdots R_{N-1,0}(u-h/2)\left(r_{22}\ket{\downarrow_0}\otimes\ket{\Uparrow}+r_{32}\ket{\uparrow_0}\otimes\sigma_{N}^{-}\ket{\Uparrow}\right)

\displaystyle=r_{22}\bra{\uparrow_0}R_{1,0}(u-h/2)\cdots R_{N-1,0}(u-h/2)\ket{\downarrow_0}\otimes\ket{\Uparrow}+r_{11}^{N-1}r_{32}\ket{\uparrow_0}\otimes\sigma_{N}^{-}\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}r_{11}^{N-1-k}r_{32}r_{22}^{k}\sigma_{N-k}^{-}\ket{\Uparrow},

so

\displaystyle C(u)B(u)\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}r_{11}^{N-1-k}r_{32}r_{22}^{k}\bra{\downarrow_0}R_{0,N}(u-h/2)\cdots R_{0,1}(u-h/2)\ket{\uparrow_0}\otimes\sigma_{N-k}^{-}\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}r_{11}^{N-1-k}r_{32}r_{33}^{k}r_{22}^{k}r_{32}r_{11}^{N-1-k}\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sinh^{2}(h){\rm e}^{-2u+h}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\sinh(u+h/2)^{2N-2k-2}\sinh(u-h/2)^{2k}\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)-\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)}{\sinh^{2}(u+h/2)-\sinh^{2}(u-h/2)}\sinh^{2}(h){\rm e}^{-2u+h}\ket{\Uparrow}.

Using the explicit expression of
K(u)
(
3.8
) and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)
(
3.15
) as well as (
3.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\Delta(u)\ket{\Uparrow},

(3.26)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\Delta(-u)\ket{\Uparrow},

(3.27)

where

\Delta(u)=1-\mu\frac{\sinh(u-h/2)\sinh(u+h\alpha-h/2)}{\sinh(h)\sinh(h\alpha)}.

(3.28)

Finally, following the standard algebraic Bethe ansatz procedure
[
58
]
, we use the commutation relations (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) to compute
t_{b}(u)\ket{\{v_m\}}
. We have from (
3.16
) and (
3.26
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\ket{\{v_m\}}={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\mathcal{B}(v_{1})\cdots\mathcal{B}(v_{M})\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m-h)\sinh(u+v_m-h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}\mathcal{B}(v_{k})\right)\mathcal{A}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{k=1}^{M}f_{1}(u,v_{k})\mathcal{B}(u)

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\left(\prod_{m\neq k}\frac{\sinh(v_k-v_m-h)\sinh(v_k+v_m-h)}{\sinh(v_k-v_m)\sinh(v_k+v_m)}\mathcal{B}(v_{k})\right)\mathcal{A}(v_{k})\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{k=1}^{M}f_{2}(u,v_{k})\mathcal{B}(u)

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\left(\prod_{m\neq k}\frac{\sinh(v_k-v_m+h)\sinh(v_k+v_m+h)}{\sinh(v_k-v_m)\sinh(v_k+v_m)}\mathcal{B}(v_{k})\right)\bar{\mathcal{D}}(v_{k})\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\Delta(u)\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m-h)\sinh(u+v_m-h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}\ket{\{v_m\}}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{k=1}^{M}f_{1}(u,v_{k})\sinh^{2N}(v_{k}+h/2)\Delta(v_{k})

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\prod_{m\neq k}\frac{\sinh(v_k-v_m-h)\sinh(v_k+v_m-h)}{\sinh(v_k-v_m)\sinh(v_k+v_m)}\ket{\psi_k}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{k=1}^{M}f_{2}(u,v_{k})\sinh^{2N}(v_{k}-h/2)\Delta(-v_{k})

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\prod_{m\neq k}\frac{\sinh(v_k-v_m+h)\sinh(v_k+v_m+h)}{\sinh(v_k-v_m)\sinh(v_k+v_m)}\ket{\psi_k}

(3.29)

and similarly from (
3.17
) and (
3.27
)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\ket{\{v_m\}}={}

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\mathcal{B}(v_{1})\cdots\mathcal{B}(v_{M})\ket{\Uparrow}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\Delta(-u)\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m+h)\sinh(u+v_m+h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}\ket{\{v_m\}}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{k=1}^{M}g_{1}(u,v_{k})\sinh^{2N}(v_{k}-h/2)\Delta(-v_{k})

\displaystyle{}\quad\times\prod_{m\neq k}\frac{\sinh(v_k-v_m+h)\sinh(v_k+v_m+h)}{\sinh(v_k-v_m)\sinh(v_k+v_m)}\ket{\psi_k}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{k=1}^{M}g_{2}(u,v_{k})\sinh^{2N}(v_{k}+h/2)\Delta(v_{k})

\displaystyle{}\quad\times\prod_{m\neq k}\frac{\sinh(v_k-v_m-h)\sinh(v_k+v_m-h)}{\sinh(v_k-v_m)\sinh(v_k+v_m)}\ket{\psi_k},

(3.30)

where

\ket{\psi_k}:=\mathcal{B}(u)\prod_{m\neq k}\mathcal{B}(v_{k})\ket{\Uparrow}.

Therefore, using (
3.20
),

t_{b}(u)\ket{\{v_m\}}=\left(\frac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u)}\mathcal{A}(u)+\frac{\sinh(2u-h)}{\sinh(2u)}\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\right)\ket{\{v_m\}}=\Lambda_{b}(u)\ket{\{v_m\}}+\ket{\psi},

where

\displaystyle\Lambda_{b}(\{v_{m}\};u)={}

\displaystyle\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\Delta(u)\frac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u)}\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m-h)\sinh(u+v_m-h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}

\displaystyle{}+\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\Delta(-u)\frac{\sinh(2u-h)}{\sinh(2u)}

\displaystyle\quad{}\times\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m+h)\sinh(u+v_m+h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}

(3.31)

and
\ket{\psi}
is a linear combination of the vectors
\{\ket{\psi_k}\}_{1\leq k\leq M}
. Moreover, (
3.29
) and (
3.30
) together with (
3.19
) imply that
\ket{\psi}
vanishes if and only if
\{v_{m}\}_{1\leq m\leq M}
satisfy the Bethe ansatz equations (BAE)

\frac{\Delta(v_{m})}{\Delta(-v_{m})}\left(\frac{\sinh(v_m+h/2)}{\sinh(v_m-h/2)}\right)^{2N}=\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{M}\frac{\sinh(v_m-v_k+h)\sinh(v_m+v_k+h)}{\sinh(v_m-v_k-h)\sinh(v_m+v_k-h)}

(3.32)

for all
1\leq m\leq M
. Thus
\ket{\{v_m\}}
is an eigenvector of
t_{b}(u)
with eigenvalue
\Lambda_{b}(\{v_{m}\};u)
for any solution of (
3.32
). Note the additional factor of
\Delta(v_{m})/\Delta(-v_{m})
compared to the BAE of the usual open
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant XXZ spin chain. It contains all the contribution of the new boundary coupling.

From (
3.11
) and (
3.31
), the energy corresponding to a solution
\{v_{m}\}_{1\leq i\leq M}
is then given by

E_{b}(\{v_{m}\})=-\mu+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh^{2}(h)}{\sinh(v_m-h/2)\sinh(v_m+h/2)}.

(3.33)

It is also possible to introduce the variables
x:=\frac{\sinh(u+h/2)}{\sinh(u-h/2)}
and
\lambda:=x+x^{-1}
to rewrite the BAE (
3.32
) as (recall that
\delta:=[2]_{\mathfrak{q}}
and
y:=\frac{[\alpha+1]_{\mathfrak{q}}}{[\alpha]_{\mathfrak{q}}}
)

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{m}-\delta+\mu\big((\delta-y)x_{m}^{-1}-1\big)}{\lambda_{m}-\delta+\mu\big((\delta-y)x_{m}-1\big)}x_{m}^{2N}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{M}\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}-\delta x_{m}(\lambda_{k}-\delta)-2\delta}{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}-\delta x_{m}^{-1}(\lambda_{k}-\delta)-2\delta}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{M}\frac{(1-\delta x_{m}+x_{m}x_{k})\big(1-\delta x_{k}+x_{k}x_{m}^{-1}\big)}{\big(1-\delta x_{m}^{-1}+x_{m}^{-1}x_{k}\big)(1-\delta x_{k}+x_{k}x_{m})}

(3.34)

for
1\leq m\leq M
. The associated energy (
3.33
) is then simply

E_{b}(\{\lambda_{m}\})=-\mu+\sum_{m=1}^{M}(\lambda_{m}-\delta).

These equations were already derived in
[
20
]
using coordinate Bethe ansatz.

A natural question is whether they “completely” describe the spectrum of
H_{b}
. To clarify what this means, we have to factor out the obvious redundancies of these equations. First, equations (
3.32
) as well as the corresponding eigenvalues (
3.31
) are invariant under permutations of the
v_{k}
, so we should consider solutions as unordered tuples
\{v_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq M}
. This of course is just a direct consequence of the commutation relation (
3.14
) and of the definition of
\ket{\{v_m\}}
in (
3.21
). Second, note that if
\{v_{k}\}
is a solution, then so are
\{v_{1},\ldots,-v_{l},\ldots,v_{M}\}
and
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}+{\rm i}\pi r,\ldots,v_{M}\}
,
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, and with the same energy for any
1\leq l\leq M
. This means that we can look for non-zero solutions in the fundamental domain

S_{+}:=\{v_{k}\,|\,{\rm Re}~v_{k}\geq 0,-\pi/2\leq{\rm Im}~v_{k}<\pi/2\}\backslash\{0\}

(3.35)

for all
1\leq k\leq M
.
16
16

16

Solutions with
v_{k}=0
for some
k
have to be excluded too because they correspond to “double roots” of the BAE. For generic values of the parameters this situation does not occur.
Finally, note that if
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{M}\}
is a solution then so is
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{M},\infty\}
and they both have the same energy. This is actually a consequence of the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
symmetry. Indeed one can show that
\mathcal{B}(\infty)\propto\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{H}_{b}}
, either by direct computation or more easily using the construction of
R(u)
from the universal
\mathsf{R}
-matrix of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, as will be explained later on at the end of Section
3.2.1
. Since
t_{b}(u)
commutes with
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, if
\ket{\{v_m\}}
is an eigenvector then so is
\mathcal{B}(\infty)\ket{\{v_m\}}\propto\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{H}_{b}}\ket{\{v_m\}}
, and moreover it has the same energy
E_{b}
because of (
3.33
). Finite solutions
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{M}\}
provide eigenstates, which we therefore expect to be
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
highest-weight vectors of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha-1+N-2M}
(
3.22
).

For
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}\notin\pm\mathfrak{q}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, using the fusion rule (
2.18
) repeatedly, we know that the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{b}
decomposes into irreducible
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules as

\mathcal{H}_{b}=\bigoplus_{M=0}^{N}{N\choose M}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha+N-2M},

so there is exactly
{N\choose M}
linearly independent highest-weight vectors in the
M
-magnon sector.
17
17

17

This can also be seen from the Schur–Weyl decomposition (
2.26
) and the dimensions of standard blob modules (
2.6
).
Therefore, we conjecture that the system of
M
equations
(
3.32
)
on an unordered set of
M
complex numbers
\{v_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq M}
, such that
v_{k}\in S_{+}
for all
1\leq k\leq M
, has exactly
{N\choose M}
distinct solutions, at least for generic values of
\alpha
and
\mu
, and that the corresponding eigenvectors
\ket{\{v_m\}}
are linearly independent and highest-weight for the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
symmetry. Then an eigenbasis of
H_{b}
is given by the vectors
\mathsf{F}^{k}_{\mathcal{H}_{b}}\ket{\{v_m\}}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Establishing such statements is usually quite challenging and rigorous proofs are known only for very few integrable spin chains
[
4
,
13
,
35
,
50
,
51
]
.

Let us also note that all the results above carry through mutatis mutandis to the root of unity cases without any obstacle.

Example 3.1
.

When
M=1
, the BAE (
3.34
) becomes

\frac{x+x^{-1}-\delta+\mu((\delta-y)x-1)}{x+x^{-1}-\delta+\mu((\delta-y)x^{-1}-1)}=x^{2N},

which can be rewritten as

U_{N}(\lambda/2)-(\mu+\delta)U_{N-1}(\lambda/2)+(1+\mu(\delta-y))U_{N-2}(\lambda/2)=0,

(3.36)

where
U_{n}
is the
n
-th Chebyshev polynomial of the second kind. The corresponding eigenvalue of
H_{b}
is

E=-\mu+\lambda-\delta.

Equation (
3.36
) has exactly
{N\choose 1}=N
solutions as it should.

When
\delta=0
(
\mathfrak{q}=i
), we recover the spectral equation from
[
14
]
. Note also that for
\delta=0
the “interaction term” on the right-hand side of (
3.34
) is always equal to unity
18
18

18

This simplification is of course not surprising as for
\delta=0
the system reduces to free fermions.
, and so the BAE will just be
M
copies of the same equation (
3.36
)
for all

M
. Denoting
(\lambda_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
the
N
solutions of (
3.36
) at
\delta=0
, any choice of
M
pairwise distinct
19
19

19

This condition is needed to ensure that the BAE are not degenerate.

\lambda_{i}
will then be a solution of the BAE. Therefore, the eigenvalues of
H_{b}
in the
M
-magnon sector are given by

E_{S}=-\mu-\sum_{i\in S}\lambda_{i}

for all sets
S\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}
of cardinality
|S|=M
, in accordance with the results of
[
14
]
. Since there are
{N\choose M}
such sets
S
, this also means that the BAE (
3.34
) have exactly
{N\choose M}
solutions for this special value of
\delta
, as we conjectured.

3.2
The two-boundary system

We now turn to the two-boundary Hamiltonian
H_{2b}
. In principle, this case can also be treated using Sklyanin’s boundary Bethe ansatz formalism. For this, in addition to the “left”
K
-matrix (
3.8
) one also needs a “right”
K
-matrix satisfying an analogue of the bYBE (
3.5
) and implementing the desired integrable boundary conditions on the right. It is possible to find such a
K
-matrix by brute force but let us instead present an alternative and conceptually better approach. To this end, we first start by giving another construction of the left
K
-matrix (
3.8
) and then extend this approach to the two-boundary case.

3.2.1
An alternative construction of
\boldsymbol{K(u)}

The basic idea is to put the boundary site carrying the
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
representation on an equal footing with the bulk sites, each of which carries a
\mathbb{C}^{2}
representation, by making apparent that their contribution to the transfer matrix (
3.20
) just comes from the same universal affine
R
-matrix, but evaluated in different representations of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.
20
20

20

More precisely, in evaluation representations of the affine quantum group
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
corresponding to different representations of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

Computing such an evaluation in full generality is a hard task, complicated by the fact that we have to choose the correct gauge so that it is compatible with our conventions
[
9
]
. However, if one of the factors of the affine
\mathsf{R}
-matrix is evaluated in the fundamental
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-representation – as will always be the case in our construction – then there is a simpler procedure using the so-called “baxterisation” trick
[
42
]
.

Concretely, recall that
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
admits a universal (non-affine)
R
-matrix given by
[
26
]
(see also
[
12
, Chapter 6.4]
)

\mathsf{R}=\mathfrak{q}^{\frac{\mathsf{H}\otimes\mathsf{H}}{2}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\{1\}^{2k}}{\{k\}!}\mathfrak{q}^{k(k-1)/2}\mathsf{E}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{F}^{k},

(3.37)

where

\{n\}!:=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\{k\}.

Although strictly speaking
\mathsf{R}\notin U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, it can be evaluated on the tensor product of any pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of representations of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
as long as
at least one
of them is finite-dimensional. We denote this evaluation by
\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}
. One of the essential properties of
\mathsf{R}
is that for any two such representations
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, the two operators

P_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\circ P_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}},

where

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}\colon\ \mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{X},

\displaystyle x\otimes y

\displaystyle\mapsto y\otimes x

is the operator permuting the two tensor factors, commute with the action of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. In other words,
\mathsf{R}
generates two (a priori different)
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners between
\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{X}
. These are precisely the building blocks we need to evaluate the affine
R
-matrix. Indeed, introducing for any representation
\mathcal{X}
of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
,

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u):=\dfrac{{\rm e}^{u+\frac{h}{2}}}{2}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}-\dfrac{{\rm e}^{-u-\frac{h}{2}}}{2}P_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\circ P_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}},

\displaystyle R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}(u):=\dfrac{{\rm e}^{u+\frac{h}{2}}}{2}\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}-\dfrac{{\rm e}^{-u-\frac{h}{2}}}{2}P_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{-1}\circ P_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}},

(3.38)

as well as

\check{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u):=P_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}\circ R_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u),\qquad\check{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}(u):=P_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}\circ R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}(u)

one checks that

(i)

R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u)=R(u)
from (
3.1
),

(ii)

\check{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u)
and
\check{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}(u)
are
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners,

(iii)

For any three representations
\mathcal{X}_{1}
,
\mathcal{X}_{2}
,
\mathcal{X}_{3}
of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
with at least two of them isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
the generalisation of the YBE (
3.3
)

R_{\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}}(u-v)R_{\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{3}}(u)R_{\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{X}_{3}}(v)=R_{\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{X}_{3}}(v)R_{\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{3}}(u)R_{\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}}(u-v)

(3.39)

is satisfied.

This means that
R_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u)
and
R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}(u)
are precisely the evaluations of the universal affine
R
-matrix we are looking for. Note also that
