R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}(u)R_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(-u)=\frac{1}{4}\left(\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{X}}-2\cosh(2u)\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}},

(3.40)

where
\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{X}}
is the Casimir (
2.32
) of
\mathcal{X}
. In particular, for
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
, by (
2.34
)

R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(-u)=\sinh(\frac{h\alpha}{2}+u)\sinh(\frac{h\alpha}{2}-u)\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}},

(3.41)

so
R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}(u)
and
R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}(u)
are invertible for
u\neq\pm h\alpha/2
.

Now going back to the construction in (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), we see that the simplest transfer matrix with an integrable
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant boundary coupling to some
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-module
\mathcal{X}
one can construct is of the form

t_{b}(u)\propto\operatorname{qtr}_{0}{T_{b}(u-h/2)\hat{T}_{b}(u-h/2)}

(3.42)

with

\displaystyle T_{b}(u):=R_{0,N}(u)\cdots R_{0,1}(u)R_{0,\mathcal{X}}(u-\zeta),

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{b}(u):=R_{\mathcal{X},0}(u+\zeta)R_{1,0}(u)\cdots R_{N,0}(u),

(3.43)

where the index
i\in\{0,\ldots,N\}
stands for the
i
-th
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-site, and
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
is some inhomogeneity parameter. Taking
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}
and comparing (
3.9
) with (
3.42
), we see that we should have

K(u)\propto R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}(u-\zeta)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha},0}(u+\zeta).

(3.44)

From this form of the
K
-matrix one can easily show that it satisfies the bYBE (
3.5
) using the YBE (
3.39
) and (
3.7
). This is actually another instance of the general result
[
58
, Proposition 2]
, simply because the YBE (
3.39
) implies that
R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}(u-\zeta)
satisfies the RTT relation (
3.12
),
R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha},0}(u+\zeta)\propto R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}(-u-\zeta)^{-1}
by (
3.41
), and
\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
is a solution of the bYBE (
3.5
) by (
3.7
).

It only remains to fix the normalisation factor in (
3.44
) and to express the coupling constant
\mu
in terms of the inhomogeneity
\zeta
. Computing (
3.44
) explicitly using (
3.37
) and (
3.38
) and matching the result with (
3.8
), we obtain

K(u)=\dfrac{R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}(u-\zeta)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u+\zeta)}{\sinh(\frac{h\alpha}{2}-\zeta)\sinh(\frac{h\alpha}{2}+\zeta)}

(3.45)

with

\mu=\dfrac{\sinh(h)\sinh(h\alpha)}{\sinh(\zeta-\frac{h\alpha}{2})\sinh(\zeta+\frac{h\alpha}{2})}.

A fruitful consequence of this formalism is that we are now able to compute
\mathcal{B}(\infty)
very easily. Indeed, define

\mathcal{T}(\infty)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(\infty)&\mathcal{B}(\infty)\\
\mathcal{C}(\infty)&\mathcal{D}(\infty)\end{pmatrix}:=\lim_{u\to+\infty}{\rm e}^{-(2N+2)u}\mathcal{T}(u).

(3.46)

Then by (
3.38
) and (
3.43
) this limit is finite and

\mathcal{T}(\infty)\propto\mathsf{R}_{0,N}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{0,1}\mathsf{R}_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha},0}\mathsf{R}_{1,0}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{N,0}.

(3.47)

But for any representations
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{Y}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{Y}}&\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}\\
0&\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{Y}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}&\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\\
0&\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{Y}}&\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathsf{K}^{-1}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right)\\
0&\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{Y}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}}&\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}}\\
0&\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}}\end{pmatrix}=\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}},

(3.48)

where we used the coproduct formula (
2.15
). Similarly,

\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{C}^{2}}=\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{C}^{2}}.

(3.49)

Finally,

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}

\displaystyle{}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}&\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\\
0&\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}&0\\
\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{X}}&\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{X}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle{}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{X}}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\{1\}^{2}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{X}}&\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\{1\}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\\
\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{-1}\mathsf{E}&\mathsf{K}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

(3.50)

where we used the defining relations of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
(
2.14
). Therefore, using (
3.48
) and (
3.49
) iteratively on (
3.47
), we have

\mathcal{T}(\infty)\propto\mathsf{R}_{0,N}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{0,1}\mathsf{R}_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha},0}\mathsf{R}_{1,0}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{N,0}=\mathsf{R}_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N},0}=\mathsf{R}_{0,\mathcal{H}_{b}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{H}_{b},0}

and so from (
3.50
) applied to
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{H}_{b}
, we obtain
\mathcal{B}(\infty)\propto\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{H}_{b}}
. The proportionality constant can easily be fixed but we will not need it. Note that the reasoning above applies to any integrable
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant spin chain as long as we renormalise the corresponding monodromy
\mathcal{T}(u)
by an appropriate power of
{\rm e}^{-u}
in (
3.46
) to make the
u\to+\infty
limit finite.

The result that
\mathcal{B}(\infty)\propto\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{H}_{b}}
, which we have now established, was used in the arguments given towards the end of Section
3.1
.

3.2.2
Bethe ansatz for
\boldsymbol{H_{2b}}

From all the above, it is now clear how to proceed to construct the transfer matrix for the two-boundary system. We simply take

t_{2b}(u):=\dfrac{\operatorname{qtr}_{0}{T_{2b}(u-h/2)\hat{T}_{2b}(u-h/2)}}{\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_l}{2}-\zeta_l)\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_l}{2}+\zeta_l)\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}-\zeta_r)\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}+\zeta_r)}

(3.51)

with

\displaystyle T_{2b}(u):=R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,N}(u)\cdots R_{0,1}(u)R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}}(u-\zeta_{l}),

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{2b}(u):=R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}},0}(u+\zeta_{l})R_{1,0}(u)\cdots R_{N,0}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r}).

(3.52)

Using (
3.9
), (
3.10
), (
3.41
)–(
3.45
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\bigg\rvert_{u=\frac{h}{2}}t_{2b}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\bigg\rvert_{u=0}\frac{\operatorname{qtr}_{0}R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(-\zeta_{r})T(u)K(u)\hat{T}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(\zeta_{r})}{\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}-\zeta_r)\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}+\zeta_r)}

\displaystyle+\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\bigg\rvert_{u=0}\frac{\operatorname{qtr}_{0}R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})T(0)K(0)\hat{T}(0)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})}{\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}-\zeta_r)\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}+\zeta_r)}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\bigg\rvert_{u=\frac{h}{2}}t_{b}(u)+4\sinh^{2N}(h)\coth(h)\left(-\mu_{r}b_{r}+\frac{\mu_{r}}{2}\right),

so (compare with (
3.11
))

\displaystyle H_{2b}=-\mu_{l}b_{l}-\mu_{r}b_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}e_{i}

\displaystyle\hphantom{H_{2b}}{}=\frac{\tanh(h)}{4\sinh^{2N}(h)}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\bigg\rvert_{u=\frac{h}{2}}t_{2b}(u)-N\cosh(h)+\frac{1}{2\cosh(h)}-\frac{\mu_{l}+\mu_{r}}{2}

(3.53)

with

\mu_{l/r}=\dfrac{\sinh(h)\sinh(h\alpha_{l/r})}{\sinh(\zeta_{l/r}-\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2})\sinh(\zeta_{l/r}+\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2})}.

(3.54)

To find the BAE one does not need to redo all the computations of the previous section. Indeed, using the YBE (
3.39
) with appropriate choices of representations
\mathcal{X}_{1}
,
\mathcal{X}_{2}
,
\mathcal{X}_{3}
and spectral parameters
u
,
v
, we have

\displaystyle R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,i}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},i}(\zeta_{r})=R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},i}(\zeta_{r})R_{0,i}(u)R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r}),

\displaystyle R_{i,0}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},i}(\zeta_{r})=R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},i}(\zeta_{r})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})R_{i,0}(u),

(3.55)

which we can use to bring the sites
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
together to the left by means of a similarity transformation. Concretely (
3.55
) for
i=N
implies that

\displaystyle T_{2b}(u)\hat{T}_{2b}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},N}(\zeta_{r})

\displaystyle=R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,N}(u)\cdots R_{N,0}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},N}(\zeta_{r})

\displaystyle=R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,N}(u)\cdots R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},N}(\zeta_{r})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})R_{N,0}(u)

\displaystyle=R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,N}(u)R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},N}(\zeta_{r})\cdots R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})R_{N,0}(u)

\displaystyle=R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},N}(\zeta_{r})R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,N}(u)\cdots R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})R_{N,0}(u),

so setting

\mathcal{R}:=R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},N}(\zeta_{r})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},N-1}(\zeta_{r})\cdots R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},2}(\zeta_{r})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},1}(\zeta_{r}),

we have
21
21

21

Note that by (
3.41
)
\mathcal{R}
is invertible if and only if
\zeta_{r}\neq\pm h\alpha_{r}/2
, but this is just equivalent to requiring that
\mu_{r}
(
3.54
) is finite.

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-1}T_{2b}(u)\hat{T}_{2b}(u)\mathcal{R}=T(u)R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}}(u-\zeta_{l})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}},0}(u+\zeta_{l})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathcal{R}^{-1}T_{2b}(u)\hat{T}_{2b}(u)\mathcal{R} =}{}\times R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})\hat{T}(u)

(3.56)

with
T(u)
,
\hat{T}(u)
from (
3.10
). Introducing the new
K
-matrix

\tilde{K}(u)=\dfrac{R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}(u-\zeta_{r})R_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}}(u-\zeta_{l})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}},0}(u+\zeta_{l})R_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}(u+\zeta_{r})}{\sinh\big(\frac{h\alpha_l}{2}-\zeta_l\big)\sinh\big(\frac{h\alpha_l}{2}+\zeta_l\big)\sinh\big(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}-\zeta_r\big)\sinh\big(\frac{h\alpha_r}{2}+\zeta_r\big)}

(3.57)

satisfying the bYBE (
3.5
) (again as a consequence of the YBE (
3.39
) and (
3.7
) or
[
58
, Proposition 2]
), the corresponding monodromy

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}(u):=T(u-h/2)\tilde{K}(u-h/2)\hat{T}(u-h/2),

and transfer matrix

\tilde{t}_{2b}(u):=\operatorname{qtr}_{0}{\tilde{\mathcal{T}}(u)},

we have by (
3.56
)

t_{2b}(u)=\mathcal{R}\tilde{t}_{2b}(u)\mathcal{R}^{-1},

so
\tilde{t}_{2b}(u)
has the same spectrum as
t_{2b}(u)
. But diagonalising
\tilde{t}_{2b}(u)
is straightforward. Indeed, to implement ABA for the one-boundary system we only needed the commutation relations (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) and the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}(u)
(
3.26
) and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)
(
3.27
) when acting on the reference state
\ket{\Uparrow}
. Replacing
K(u)
by
\tilde{K}(u)
does not change the commutation relations (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) because they were solely derived from the bYBE (
3.13
) which the new monodromy
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}(u)
equally satisfies as
\tilde{K}(u)
is also a solution of the bYBE (
3.5
). Therefore, we only need to compute the new eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}(u)
and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)
when acting on the new reference state
22
22

22

We use the same notations as for the one-boundary case for the entries of the new monodromy
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}(u)
and the new reference state
\ket{0}\otimes\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes N}
.

\ket{\Uparrow}:=\ket{0}\otimes\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes N}.

This amounts to replacing the diagonal coefficients
a(u)
and
d(u)
of
K(u)
in (
3.23
)–(
3.25
) by the diagonal coefficients
\tilde{a}(u)
and
\tilde{d}(u)
of
\tilde{K}(u)
(which are now operators acting on
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
) in all the computations of Section
3.1
.
23
23

23

The trick we just used is not essential to implement ABA in the two-boundary case and we could have equally worked with
t_{2b}(u)
directly (see Appendix
B
).
Doing so, we obtain, instead of (
3.26
) and (
3.27
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\Delta_{l}(u)\Delta_{r}(u)\ket{\Uparrow},

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\ket{\Uparrow}=\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\Delta_{l}(-u)\Delta_{r}(-u)\ket{\Uparrow},

while (
3.28
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\Delta_{l/r}(u)

\displaystyle=1-\mu_{l/r}\frac{\sinh(u-h/2)\sinh(u+h(\alpha_{l/r}-1/2))}{\sinh(h)\sinh(h\alpha_{l/r})}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sinh(u+h\frac{\alpha_{l/r}-1}{2}-\zeta_{l/r})\sinh(u+h\frac{\alpha_{l/r}-1}{2}+\zeta_{l/r})}{\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2}-\zeta_{l/r})\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2}+\zeta_{l/r})}.

(3.58)

Thus

\ket{\{v_m\}}=\mathcal{B}(v_{1})\cdots\mathcal{B}(v_{M})\ket{\Uparrow}

is an eigenvector of
\tilde{t}_{2b}(u)
– or, equivalently,
\mathcal{R}\ket{\{v_m\}}
is an eigenvector of
t_{2b}(u)
– with eigenvalue (compare with (
3.31
))

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2b}(\{v_{m}\};u)=\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\Delta_{l}(u)\Delta_{r}(u)\frac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u)}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda_{2b}(\lx@text@lbrace v_m\lx@text@rbrace;u)=+}{}\times\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m-h)\sinh(u+v_m-h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda_{2b}(\lx@text@lbrace v_m\lx@text@rbrace;u)=}{}+\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\Delta_{l}(-u)\Delta_{r}(-u)\frac{\sinh(2u-h)}{\sinh(2u)}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Lambda_{2b}(\lx@text@lbrace v_m\lx@text@rbrace;u)=+}{}\times\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m+h)\sinh(u+v_m+h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}

(3.59)

if and only if
\{v_{m}\}_{1\leq m\leq M}
satisfy the Bethe ansatz equations (compare with (
3.32
))

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{l}(v_{m})\Delta_{r}(v_{m})}{\Delta_{l}(-v_{m})\Delta_{r}(-v_{m})}\!\left(\frac{\sinh(v_m+h/2)}{\sinh(v_m-h/2)}\right)^{2N}\!\!=\!\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{M}\frac{\sinh(v_m-v_k+h)\sinh(v_m+v_k+h)}{\sinh(v_m-v_k-h)\sinh(v_m+v_k-h)}

(3.60)

for all
1\leq m\leq M
. The corresponding eigenvalue of
H_{2b}
is (compare with (
3.33
))

E_{2b}(\{v_{m}\})=-\mu_{l}-\mu_{r}+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh^{2}(h)}{\sinh(v_m-h/2)\sinh(v_m+h/2)}.

Of course we could have guessed this result on physical grounds by interpreting
\Delta(u)/\Delta(-u)
as the phase acquired by a quasi-particle of rapidity
u
reflected at the boundary. For periodic integrable spin chains, this heuristic can actually be mathematically justified using the representation theory of the affine quantum group
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
(the algebra defined by the RTT relation (
3.12
)), establishing that the form of the eigenvalues of the transfer matrix and the BAE are completely fixed by the choice of a (trigonometric) Drinfeld polynomial, which uniquely specifies (up to isomorphism) an irreducible highest-weight representation of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
for the physical space
[
11
]
. This allows to implement ABA for any such choice of representation without almost any computation. To the best of our knowledge, a similar formalism has not been fully developed for open
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant spin chains, especially for infinite-dimensional highest-weight representations, so for the sake of completeness we perform ABA for all open integrable
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant highest-weight spin chains in Appendix
B
. This also provides an alternative derivation of eigenvalues (
3.59
) and BAE (
3.60
) which does not require
K
-matrices nor the similarity transformation (
3.56
).

Coming back to the two-boundary case, we have
\mathcal{B}(\infty)\propto\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}
,
24
24

24

As was mentioned at the end of Section
3.2.1
, this is true for any integrable
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant spin chain. Note also that for
{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}
we need to renormalise the monodromy
\mathcal{T}(u)
by
{\rm e}^{-(2N+4)u}
in (
3.46
) to make the limit
u\to+\infty
finite.
and we expect the finite (permutation invariant) solutions
\{v_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq M}
of the BAE (
3.60
) belonging to the fundamental domain
S_{+}
(
3.35
) to provide all the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
highest-weight eigenstates of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-2+N-2M}
of
H_{2b}
. Recalling the decomposition (
2.36
), valid for
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l/r}},\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}}\neq\pm\mathfrak{q}^{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\mathcal{H}_{2b}=\bigoplus_{M\geq 0}\mathcal{H}_{M}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M}

and the dimensions
d_{M}
(
2.37
), there are
d_{M}
such linearly independent vectors and so for generic values of the parameters we conjecture that the BAE (
3.60
) have
d_{M}
such solutions. Note in particular that the number of magnons
M
is not bounded as in the one-boundary case.

The BAE (
3.60
) are exactly the ones found in
[
10
,
54
]
for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
(
1.1
) under the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
), which is not surprising: indeed, by Corollary
2.4
(for
N
even),
H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}
is an irreducible subblock of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
for
0\leq M\leq N-1
and
H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}=H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
for
M\geq N
. Note however that our derivation of the BAE is fully rigorous and relies on no additional assumptions. Also, we are now able to pinpoint
the algebraic origin of the Nepomechie condition: it is just a direct consequence of the fusion rules (
2.18
) and more importantly (
2.35
), yielding the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-decomposition of
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
(
2.36
) and restricting the generator
\mathsf{Y}
(
2.33
) of the two-boundary TL algebra to take exactly the values
Y_{M}
(
2.38
) of the Nepomechie condition in its irreducible sectors
\mathcal{H}_{M}
.
Strictly speaking, this relation between
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
and
H_{2b}
is only valid for generic values of
\mathfrak{q}
and
\alpha_{l/r}
, as our arguments are based on the Schur–Weyl duality from Theorem
2.3
, proven under this assumption. However, by continuity of the (generalised) spectrum, and since the “allowed” values of the parameters form a dense set, the
spectral
equivalence between
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
and (sectors of)
H_{2b}
still holds for all
\mathfrak{q},\alpha_{l/r}\in\mathbb{C}
even though their exact algebraic connection may be more involved in the non-generic non-semi-simple cases. An instance where ABA was applied to such a case can be found in
[
32
]
.

3.2.3
Completeness

Another interesting consequence of our formalism is the question of completeness of the BAE for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
. For
M\geq N
,
H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}=H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
so it is equivalent for both Hamiltonians. For
0\leq M\leq N-1
, however,
H_{2b}|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}
is only an irreducible subblock of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
by Corollary
2.4
, but both Hamiltonians still have the same BAE. This means that for
0\leq M\leq N-1
, the BAE (
3.60
) cannot possibly provide all the eigenvalues of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
, as is already quite clear for
M=0
. But thanks to Corollary
2.5
, we know exactly which additional BAE we have to write to diagonalise the remaining block of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
for
0\leq M\leq N-1
: we simply need to replace
H_{2b}
by
\overline{H}_{2b}
, which just amounts to the replacement (
2.42
), namely
\alpha_{l/r},\mu_{l/r}\to-\alpha_{l/r},-\mu_{l/r}
up to an irrelevant additive constant, and by (
2.43
) the simultaneous replacement of the magnon number
M
by the dual magnon number
\overline{M}:=N-M-1
. Thus (
3.58
) is now replaced by

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}_{l/r}(u)

\displaystyle=:1-\mu_{l/r}\frac{\sinh(u-h/2)\sinh(u-h(\alpha_{l/r}+1/2))}{\sinh(h)\sinh(h\alpha_{l/r})}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sinh(u-h\frac{\alpha_{l/r}+1}{2}-\zeta_{l/r})\sinh(u-h\frac{\alpha_{l/r}+1}{2}+\zeta_{l/r})}{\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2}-\zeta_{l/r})\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_{l/r}}{2}+\zeta_{l/r})},

and we obtain the BAE

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\Delta}_{l}(v_{m})\overline{\Delta}_{r}(v_{m})}{\overline{\Delta}_{l}(-v_{m})\overline{\Delta}_{r}(-v_{m})}\!\left(\frac{\sinh(v_m+h/2)}{\sinh(v_m-h/2)}\right)^{2N}\!\!=\!\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{\overline{M}}\frac{\sinh(v_m-v_k+h)\sinh(v_m+v_k+h)}{\sinh(v_m-v_k-h)\sinh(v_m+v_k-h)}

(3.61)

for
\overline{M}
Bethe roots
\{v_{m}\}_{1\leq m\leq\overline{M}}
. Together with (
3.60
) we expect (
3.61
) to provide the complete spectrum of
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
for
0\leq M\leq N-1
, as was previously conjectured in
[
55
]
(see also
[
20
]
). Rigorously proving such a completeness statement is of course very hard and has only been achieved for a handful of integrable models
[
13
,
51
]
. Still, our approach demonstrates that two sets of BAE are definitely needed and explains their algebraic origin. Note also that by Corollary
2.5

(iv)
, (
3.61
) are the BAE for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}^{(M)}
with
M\leq-1
. Thus (
3.60
) and (
3.61
) taken together cover all the possible values of the parameters satisfying the Nepomechie constraint (
1.3
) for
M\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Finally, let us also mention that there exists a different set of BAE for the two-boundary which can be established using functional relations between various
Q
-functions
[
60
]
or separation of variables
[
44
]
. These equations are rather different from (
3.60
) and it is therefore quite surprising that they should yield the same spectrum. We discuss this question in more detail in Appendix
C
.

Remark 3.2
.

If
\mathfrak{q}
is a
2p
-th root of unity, the fusion rule (
2.35
) becomes

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}\cong\bigoplus_{n=0}^{p-1}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}+p-1-2n},

so we have

\mathcal{H}_{2b}=\bigoplus_{M=0}^{N+p-1}d_{M}^{\prime}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}+p-1+N-2M}

with the magnon number
0\leq M\leq N+p-1
now bounded and some different multiplicities
d^{\prime}_{M}
whose explicit expression can be found in
[
14
]
. Thus we see that contrary to the one-boundary case where we expect the same number of solutions to the BAE (
3.32
) for generic and root of unity
\mathfrak{q}
, in the two-boundary case the BAE (
3.60
) should apparently behave quite differently in these two situations. This must also be related to the fact that the representation theory of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
changes significantly at roots of unity and in particular the structure of the vacuum module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
– which is still not fully known – becomes much more complicated than for generic
\mathfrak{q}
as in Theorem
2.2
(see also
[
14
, Conjecture 2]
). This requires further study.

4
Outlook

In this paper, we have constructed a
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant realisation
H_{2b}
of the open XXZ Hamiltonian with non-diagonal boundary terms
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
for all values of the parameters satisfying (
1.3
) by using the representation theory of the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
. This enabled us to rigorously derive the BAE equations (
3.60
) for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
by ABA and to understand the algebraic origin of the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
) from the point of view of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
fusion rules (
2.18
)–(
2.35
), restricting the possible values of the weight
Y
of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
to the discrete set
\{Y_{M},M\in\mathbb{Z}\}
(
2.38
).

Although the BAE we derived were previously known in the literature, a direct construction of the eigenstates by standard algebraic Bethe ansatz had never been performed until now and could be most useful in the computation of finer observables of the system, such as correlation functions and form factors, and in the study of some closely related models such as the asymmetric simple exclusion process (ASEP)
[
16
,
57
]
. It is also worth mentioning that the algebraic Bethe ansatz formalism we presented generalises straightforwardly to open XXZ spin chains with additional inhomogeneity parameters at every site. Finally, our construction admits a well-defined
\mathfrak{q}\to 1
limit, giving rise to non-compact boundary conditions for the open XXX spin chain.

It would be very interesting to construct an even more general
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant spin chain which could reach arbitrary values of
Y
and not just the discrete set subject to the Nepomechie condition (
1.3
). The weights
y_{l/r}
are entirely determined by the value of the Casimir
\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l/r}}}
and the possible values of
Y
by the values
\mathsf{C}
can take on the tensor product
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
. Therefore, to find such a generalisation, one would need to construct two new one-parameter families of boundary
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules
\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{l/r}}
, with
\alpha_{l/r}
parametrising
y_{l/r}
through the Casimir
\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{l/r}}}
, and more crucially such that
\mathsf{C}
can take any value on
\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{r}}
. This implies that the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-decomposition of
\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{r}}
should no longer be discrete as in (
2.35
) but continuous. For the
\mathfrak{q}\to 1
XXX case, such fusion rules are known to arise in the context of principal series representations of
\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})

[
52
]
. For the general XXZ spin chain a natural guess would be to use their
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}

\mathfrak{q}
-deformed analogues. Such spin chains could shed light on the origin of the general BAE for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
(see Appendix
C
). Continuous fusion rules are also a central feature of Liouville CFT and the celebrated DOZZ formula for its 3-point function constants, which was recently proved
[
45
]
, including in the imaginary case
[
1
]
where its relevance for lattice models has been established
[
36
]
. A well-defined lattice model with similar properties would certainly be of great help to further our understanding of the challenging questions still surrounding this theory. We will explore these ideas in future work.

It would be of course very desirable to extend our formalism to XXZ-type chains of spin-
1
with non-diagonal integrable boundary conditions
[
37
]
and eventually to arbitrary spins
[
23
,
46
]
. This should reveal new lattice algebras generalising the two-boundary Temperley–Lieb algebra. One obvious guess would be here the fused Temperley–Lieb algebras, or fused Hecke algebras
[
15
]
for higher rank cases. Unfortunately, their boundary versions and the corresponding representation theory are poorly understood. A yet another interesting problem would be developing ABA in the non-semi-simple cases when the weights of the Verma modules take integer values, even at generic
\mathfrak{q}
, and the Hamiltonian is non-diagonalisable. This is similar to the problem studied in
[
32
]
but one should take limits in
\alpha_{l/r}
variables instead of limits
\mathfrak{q}
to a root of unity.

Finally, it is known that in the critical domain
|\mathfrak{q}|=1
, the boundary loop model defined by
\mathbf{H}
(
2.13
) is conformally invariant in the large-
N
scaling limit, with some explicit conjectures for its conformal spectrum based on the Coulomb gas approach
[
28
,
39
]
including for generalisations to anisotropic boundary conditions in the dilute
O(n)
model
[
29
,
30
]
. Using the explicit spin chain Hamiltonians
H_{b}
and
H_{2b}
and the corresponding BAE (
3.32
)–(
3.60
) one can now hope to establish these results rigorously. This will be the subject of a forthcoming paper. Also, since from a physical perspective the two-boundary system can be seen as the fusion of two one-boundary systems, the representation theory of the discrete lattice algebras
\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y,N}
and
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y,N}
may provide new insight into the fusion of Virasoro primary fields.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
2.3

The goal of this section is to prove the isomorphisms (
2.40
). By Theorem
2.2
, this would imply that all the
\mathcal{H}_{M}
appearing in the decomposition (
2.39
) are irreducible representations of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
and therefore that
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
are indeed mutual centralisers on
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
.

The main idea is to follow a more abstract approach to Schur–Weyl duality and rewrite the decomposition into irreducible
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules (
2.36
) as

\mathcal{H}_{2b}=\bigoplus_{M\geq 0}\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},

where
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
denotes the space of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners between two
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
. Of course
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)
and
\mathcal{H}_{M}
are isomorphic as vector spaces: a
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiner
f\colon\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M}\to\mathcal{H}_{2b}
is uniquely determined by the image of the highest-weight vector
\ket{0}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M}
(
2.17
), that is the choice of a highest-weight vector
f\ket{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{2b}
of the same weight. Since the subspace of all such vectors is
\mathcal{H}_{M}
by definition

\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\mathbb{C}\ket{0},\mathcal{H}_{M}\right)\cong\mathcal{H}_{M}.

(A.1)

Actually, this isomorphism is even an isomorphism of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-modules. Indeed, the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant action of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
on
\mathcal{H}_{2b}
induces an action on the target space of intertwiners
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)
which is equivalent to the action
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
on
\mathcal{H}_{M}
via the isomorphism (
A.1
).

The advantage of this rewriting is that we can construct morphisms
25
25

25

We shall use the term “morphism” instead of “homomorphism” to lighten the exposition.
between standard
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-modules and
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)
in a much more canonical way. The idea is to use the diagrammatical calculus for
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners to map well-chosen
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
-modules to some bigger spaces of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners in a way compatible with the lattice algebra action and then to appropriately specialise these maps to the spaces
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\allowbreak\mathcal{H}_{2b})
.

Let us first recall a few basic facts about this diagrammatical formalism and explain how it can be used to rederive Schur–Weyl duality for the simpler case of the TL algebra (
2.21
). To any TL diagram, that is a planar configuration of non-intersecting strings between two sets of
N
points, one can assign a
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiner from
\mathcal{H}:=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N}
to itself, that is an element of
\operatorname{End}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(\mathcal{H})
, by mapping the elementary generators of TL diagrams
e_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq N-1
, to the corresponding
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners (
2.19
). Since their composition rules are the same as the defining relations of the TL algebra (
2.1
), this provides a
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-invariant representation
\rho_{\mathsf{TL}}\colon\mathsf{TL}_{\delta,N}\to\operatorname{End}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(\mathcal{H})
.

More generally, let us denote
\mathcal{W}_{\leq j}
the vector space spanned by all planar configurations of non-intersecting strings between a set of

points (at the bottom) and a set of
N

is not an algebra, (except for

where we recover the TL algebra),

naturally acts on
\mathcal{W}_{\leq j}
by stacking TL diagrams on top of elements of
\mathcal{W}_{\leq j}
. Moreover, using the elementary building blocks

-modules

(A.5)

It is clearly non-zero as

\pi_{j}(X)=\delta^{j-N/2}X\circ\bigl(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2j}}\otimes C_{2j+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes C_{N-1}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}\bigr)

for all
X\in\imaginary(\rho_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}|_{\mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]}\big)
, from which it is clear that

•

\pi_{j}
is well-defined,

•

\pi_{j}(X)
is a
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiner as the composition of two
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners,

•

\pi_{j}
is a
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
-module morphism as
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
acts only on the target space of the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiner
X
.

Thus, for all
0\leq j\leq N/2
, we have constructed a
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
-module morphism

\Psi_{j}:=\pi_{j}\circ\rho_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}|_{\mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]}\colon\ \mathcal{W}_{\leq j}^{2b}[\mathsf{Y}]\cong\mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]\to\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\big(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\big)

(A.6)

as we wanted.

This abstractly-defined map
\Psi_{j}
has actually a very natural diagrammatical interpretation. Indeed, two-boundary TL diagrams of

Using the above procedure, we can uniquely map diagrams of

For all
\ell\in\mathcal{W}_{\leq j}^{2b}
, it is technically possible to write an explicit expression of the corresponding

-intertwiner

in terms of the

,

,
{1\leq i\leq N-1}
,

, but the shortcut we took by adding the “spectator” part

achieves the same goal faster while also providing a direct proof that

.

Coming back to our proof, let us first consider the morphism of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
-modules

\Psi_{0}\colon\ \mathcal{W}_{\leq 0}^{2b}[\mathsf{Y}]\cong\mathcal{I}_{0}[\mathsf{Y}]\to\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b}).

Note that, by definition,
\mathcal{W}_{\leq 0}^{2b}[\mathsf{Y}]=\mathcal{W}_{0}[\mathsf{Y}]
, the universal vacuum module of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
.

Recalling the fusion rule (
2.35
), rewritten in a convenient way,

\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}\cong\bigoplus_{M\geq N/2}\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},

(A.7)

we have maps
\varphi_{M}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}\right)
,
M\geq N/2
, which are unique up to normalisation. They induce maps

\Psi_{0}^{(M)}\colon\ \mathcal{W}_{0}[\mathsf{Y}]\to\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}),\qquad\ell\mapsto\Psi_{0}(\ell)\circ\varphi_{M},

which are also morphisms of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
-modules. Moreover, for any
f\!\in\!\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\!(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\allowbreak\mathcal{H}_{2b})
,
M\geq 0
,

\mathsf{Y}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}f(-)=f(\mathsf{Y}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M}}-)=Y_{M}f(-).

(A.8)

In other words,
\mathsf{Y}
acts as the scalar
Y_{M}
on
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)
which means that
{(\mathsf{Y}-Y_{M})\mathcal{W}_{0}[\mathsf{Y}]\subseteq\operatorname{Ker}\Psi_{0}^{(M)}}
and so, for all
M\geq N/2
,
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}
induces a morphism of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-modules between
\mathcal{W}_{0}[\mathsf{Y}]/(\mathsf{Y}-Y_{M})\mathcal{W}_{0}[\mathsf{Y}]\cong\mathcal{W}_{0}
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)\cong\mathcal{H}_{M}
, which we still denote
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}
.

Note that, concretely, for all
\ell\in\mathcal{W}_{0}
,
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}(\ell)\in\mathcal{H}_{M}
is simply the evaluation of the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiner
\Psi_{0}(\ell)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)
at the highest-weight vector
\ket{w_M}
of the
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M}
summand of
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
in (
A.7
). This evaluation
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}(\ell)=\Psi_{0}(\ell)\ket{w_M}
is indeed an element of
\mathcal{H}_{M}
– the subspace of highest-weight vectors of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-2+N-2M}
– simply because for all
\ell\in\mathcal{W}_{0}[\mathsf{Y}]
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\!\Psi_{0}^{(M)}\!(\ell)=\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\Psi_{0}(\ell)\!\ket{w_M}=\Psi_{0}(\ell)\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}\!\ket{w_M}=0,

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\!\Psi_{0}^{(M)}\!(\ell)=\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\Psi_{0}(\ell)\!\ket{w_M}=\Psi_{0}(\ell)\!\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}\!\ket{w_M}=\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-2+N-2M}\Psi_{0}(\ell)\!\ket{w_M}\!.

(A.9)

Clearly
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}\neq 0
as, for example,

.
.
\ldots
.
.
\mapsto~\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N/2}C_{2i-1}\neq 0.

By Theorem
2.2

(iii)
, the
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-module
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is irreducible for all
M\geq N
, so
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}
must be injective. Since
\dim\mathcal{W}_{0}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{M}=2^{N}
by (
2.9
) and (
2.37
),
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}
is an isomorphism and so
\mathcal{H}_{M}\cong\mathcal{W}_{0}
for all
M\geq N
.

Now for
N/2\leq M\leq N-1
,
\operatorname{Ker}\Psi_{0}^{(M)}
is a
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-submodule of
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and so by Theorem
2.2

(ii)
it must be equal to either
\{0\}
,
\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
or
\mathcal{W}_{0}
. It cannot be equal to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
because
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}\neq 0
. It cannot be equal to
\{0\}
because
d_{M}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{M}<\dim\mathcal{W}_{0}=2^{N}
by (

). Therefore,
\operatorname{Ker}\Psi_{0}^{(M)}=\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
and so
\Psi_{0}^{(M)}
induces a non-zero injective
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-module morphism between
\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{M}
. Since by (
2.9
) and (
2.37
)
\dim\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{M}=d_{M}
we have
\mathcal{H}_{M}\cong\mathcal{W}_{0}/\mathcal{W}_{M+1-N/2}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
for all
N/2\leq M\leq N-1
.

It remains to deal with the cases
0\leq M\leq N/2-1
. Here we can no longer use
\Psi_{0}
because the tensor product

(

) does not contain summands

for

. The idea is then to consider the above-constructed

-module morphisms (

)

\Psi_{j}\colon\ \mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]\to\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\big(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\big)

now with
1\leq j\leq N/2
. Evaluating the elements of
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\big(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\big)
at
\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\ket{0}
or, equivalently, pre-composing them with the only (up to normalisation)
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-intertwiner

\chi_{j}\colon\ \mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+2j}\to\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},

we obtain, for each
1\leq j\leq N/2
, a morphism of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},N}^{\rm uni}
-modules
29
29

29

Using the same reasoning as in (
A.4
) and (
A.9
), we easily see that
\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\Psi_{j}(\ell)\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\ket{0}=0
and
\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\Psi_{j}(\ell)\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\ket{0}=\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-2+2j}\Psi_{j}(\ell)\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\ket{0}
for all
\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]
so indeed
\imaginary\psi_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{N/2-j}
for all
1\leq j\leq N/2
.

\displaystyle\psi_{j}\colon\ \mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]\to\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+2j},\mathcal{H}_{b}\right)\cong\mathcal{H}_{N/2-j},

\displaystyle\hphantom{\psi_{j}\colon}{}\ \ell\mapsto\Psi_{j}(\ell)\circ\chi_{j}\cong\Psi_{j}(\ell)\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\ket{0}.

By (
A.8
),
\mathsf{Y}
acts as the scalar
Y_{M}
on
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)\cong\mathcal{H}_{M}
and so for all
1\leq j\leq N/2
,
\psi_{j}
induces a morphism of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-modules,
M=N/2-j
, between
\mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]/(\mathsf{Y}-Y_{M})\mathcal{I}_{j}[\mathsf{Y}]\cong\mathcal{I}_{j}
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+2j},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\right)
which we still denote
\psi_{j}
.

Now consider the subspace
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subset\mathcal{I}_{j}
spanned by all two-boundary TL diagrams
\ell
of the form
\ell^{\prime}\otimes\cap^{\otimes N/2-j}
where
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{\leq j}^{2b}
is a two-boundary TL diagram from
2j
to
N
points such that it has either

(i)

strictly less than
2j
through lines,

(ii)

or exactly
2j
through lines with the leftmost or rightmost through line of
\ell^{\prime}
carrying a left/right anti-blob.

The left action of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
on
\mathcal{I}_{j}
can only decrease the number of through lines and can never change the left/right blob/anti-blob decoration of the leftmost/rightmost through line of a diagram with
2j
through lines,
30
30

30

It is actually this property that makes the standard modules
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{bb}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{b\bar{b}}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}b}
,
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{\bar{b}\bar{b}}
well-defined.
which implies that
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is a stable
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
subspace. Moreover, by definition of standard modules, the quotient
\mathcal{I}_{j}/\mathcal{U}_{j}
– which is exactly the space of two-boundary TL diagrams with
2j
through lines with leftmost and rightmost through lines both carrying left and right blobs – is isomorphic to
\mathcal{W}_{j}^{bb}
as a
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-module.

Let us show the following.

Lemma A.1
.

For all
1\leq j\leq N/2
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subseteq\operatorname{Ker}\psi_{j}
.

Proof.

First consider
\ell\in\mathcal{U}_{j}
of the form
(i)
. This means that
\ell=\ell^{\prime}\otimes\cap^{\otimes N/2-j}
with
{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{\leq j}^{2b}}
containing at least one cap at the bottom. Moreover, at least one of these caps connects neighbouring sites: if that was not the case, it would be impossible to fill the bottom
2j
points of
\ell^{\prime}
with non-intersecting caps and through lines. If
j=1
,
\ell^{\prime}
contains no through lines and the bottom of
\ell^{\prime}
has a single cap linking points
1
and
2
which can carry any left/right blob/anti-blob configuration. Therefore,
\Psi_{1}(\ell)
is of the form
A\bar{C}_{1}b_{l}b_{r}
, or
A\bar{C}_{1}b_{l}\bar{b}_{r}
, or
A\bar{C}_{1}\bar{b}_{l}b_{r}
, or
A\bar{C}_{1}\bar{b}_{l}\bar{b}_{r}
for some
A\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b})
and so

\psi_{1}(\ell)=\Psi_{1}(\ell)\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow\uparrow}\otimes\ket{0}=0

because
\bar{C}_{1}b_{l}b_{r}\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow\uparrow}\otimes\ket{0}=\bar{C}_{1}\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow\uparrow}\otimes\ket{0}=0
and
\bar{b}_{l}\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}=\bar{b}_{r}\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{0}=0
.

Now take
j\geq 2
and first suppose
\ell^{\prime}
contains through lines. We have the following cases:

•

Either the bottom points
1
and
2j
of
\ell^{\prime}
are both occupied by through lines. Then the caps in between cannot carry blobs/anti-blobs and so
\ell^{\prime}
contains an undecorated cap linking points
i
and
i+1
for some
2\leq i\leq 2j-2
.

•

Or the bottom point
1
is connected to some other bottom point
2\leq k\leq 2j-2
by a cap (for
k
even). If
k\neq 2
, then the points strictly between
1
and
k
can only contain undecorated caps, at least one of them linking neighbouring points
i
and
i+1
for some
2\leq i\leq k-2
. If
k=2
,
\ell^{\prime}
contains a cap linking points
1
and
2
which can only carry a left blob/anti-blob, because it is separated from the right boundary by a through line.

•

Or the bottom point
2j
is connected to some other bottom point
3\leq k\leq 2j-1
by a cap (for
k
odd). If
k\neq 2j-1
, then the points strictly between
k
and
2j
can only contain undecorated caps, at least one of them linking neighbouring points
i
and
i+1
for some
k+1\leq i\leq 2j-2
. If
k=2j-1
,
\ell^{\prime}
contains a cap linking points
2j-1
and
2j
which can only carry a right blob/anti-blob, because it is separated from the left boundary by a through line.

On the other hand if
\ell^{\prime}
contains no through lines and
j\geq 2
then:

•

Either the bottom of
\ell^{\prime}
contains some “long” cap linking points
1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq 2j
with
k+3\leq k^{\prime}
(for
k
and
k^{\prime}
of opposite parities) so there is an undecorated cap linking points
i
and
i+1
for some
k+1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}-1
.

•

Or the bottom of
\ell^{\prime}
contains only nearest-neighbour caps. Then either the leftmost cap (linking points
1
and
2
) is undecorated or carries only a left blob/antiblob, or the rightmost cap (linking points
2j-1
and
2j
) is undecorated or carries only a right blob/antiblob. Indeed, the leftmost and rightmost caps cannot both carry left and right blobs/anti-blobs as they would both need to touch the two boundaries of the system, which is impossible without them crossing.

To summarise, if
j\geq 2
, the bottom of
\ell^{\prime}
contains

•

either an undecorated cap linking neighbouring points
i
and
i+1
for some
1\leq i\leq 2j
,

•

or a cap linking points
1
and
2
and carrying only a left blob/anti-blob,

•

or a cap linking points
2j-1
and
2j
and carrying only a right blob/anti-blob.

This means that
\Psi_{j}(\ell)
is of the form
A\bar{C}_{i}
, for some
1\leq i\leq 2j
, or
A\bar{C}_{1}b_{l}
, or
A\bar{C}_{1}\bar{b}_{l}
, or
A\bar{C}_{2j-1}b_{r}
, or
A\bar{C}_{2j-1}\bar{b}_{r}
for some
A\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\big(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2(j-1)}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\big)
. Therefore,

\psi_{j}(\ell)=\Psi_{j}(\ell)\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\ket{0}=0.

Finally, if
\ell
is of the form
(ii)
,
\Psi_{j}(\ell)
is of the form
A\bar{b}_{l}
or
A\bar{b}_{r}
with some
A\in\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\big(\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},\mathcal{H}_{2b}\big)
and since
\bar{b}_{l}\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}=\bar{b}_{r}\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{0}=0
,
\psi_{j}(\ell)=0
.
∎

Now
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subseteq\operatorname{Ker}\psi_{j}
implies that
\psi_{j}
induces a morphism of
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-modules

\tilde{\psi}_{j}\colon\ \mathcal{I}_{j}/\mathcal{U}_{j}\cong\mathcal{W}_{j}^{bb}\to\mathcal{H}_{M}

for all
1\leq j\leq N/2
, with
M=N/2-j
. Clearly,
\tilde{\psi}_{j}
is non-zero as, for example,

.
.
\bullet
.
.
\ldots
.
.
.
.
\ldots
.
.
.
.
\blacksquare
\mapsto~\ket{0}\otimes\ket{\uparrow}^{\otimes 2j}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i=j}^{N-1}C_{2i+1}\right)\otimes\ket{0}\neq 0.

By Theorem
2.2
, the
2\mathsf{B}_{\delta,y_{l/r},Y_{M},N}
-module
\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}
is irreducible for all
1\leq M\leq N/2-1
so
\tilde{\psi}_{j}
is injective. Since
\dim\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{M}
by (
2.9
) and (
2.37
), we have
\mathcal{W}_{N/2-M}^{bb}\cong\mathcal{H}_{M}
for all
0\leq M\leq N/2-1
which completes the proof.

Appendix B
ABA for a general
\boldsymbol{U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
-invariant highest-weight spin chain

Consider a tensor product
\mathcal{X}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{X}_{i}
of irreducible highest-weight
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha_{i}-1}
(that is,
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is a spin-
\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2}
representation if
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and a Verma module otherwise) and define the monodromy

\mathcal{T}(u):=T(u-h/2)\hat{T}(u-h/2)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}(u)&\mathcal{B}(u)\\
\mathcal{C}(u)&\mathcal{D}(u)\end{pmatrix}

with

\displaystyle T(u-h/2):=\begin{pmatrix}A(u)&B(u)\\
C(u)&D(u)\end{pmatrix}=R_{0,\mathcal{X}_{n}}(u-h/2-\zeta_{n})\cdots R_{0,\mathcal{X}_{1}}(u-h/2-\zeta_{1}),

\displaystyle\hat{T}(u-h/2):=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{A}(u)&\hat{B}(u)\\
\hat{C}(u)&\hat{D}(u)\end{pmatrix}=R_{\mathcal{X}_{1},0}(u-h/2+\zeta_{1})\cdots R_{\mathcal{X}_{n},0}(u-h/2+\zeta_{n}),

for some inhomogeneity parameters
\zeta_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and the transfer matrix

t(u):=\operatorname{qtr}_{0}\mathcal{T}(u)={\rm e}^{h}\mathcal{A}(u)+{\rm e}^{-h}\mathcal{D}(u).

By
[
58
, Proposition 2]
,
\mathcal{T}(u)
is a solution of the bYBE (
3.13
): using the YBE (
3.39
) one checks that
T(u)
satisfies the RTT relation (
3.12
),
\hat{T}(u)\propto T(-u)^{-1}
because of (
3.40
), and
\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
is a solution of the bYBE (
3.5
) by (
3.7
), so the product
\mathcal{T}(u+h/2):=T(u)\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{C}_{2}}\hat{T}(u)
is also one.
31
31

31

This statement can also be proved by induction on
n
using only the YBE (
3.39
) and (
3.7
).
Therefore, the commutation relations (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) remain the same.

It remains to compute the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}(u)
and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)
when acting on the reference state

\ket{\mathbf{0}}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\ket{0_i},

where
\ket{0_i}
is the highest-weight vector of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
. To do so, first write

\displaystyle R_{0,\mathcal{X}_{i}}(u-h/2-\zeta_{i})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\rm e}^{u-\zeta_{i}}}{2}\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}_{i}}-\dfrac{{\rm e}^{-u+\zeta_{i}}}{2}P_{\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{2}}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{2}}^{-1}\circ P_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\rm e}^{u-\zeta_{i}}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{i}&\{1\}\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{i}\mathsf{F}_{i}\\
0&\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{i}\end{pmatrix}-\dfrac{{\rm e}^{-u+\zeta_{i}}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{i}&0\\
-\{1\}\mathsf{E}_{i}\mathsf{K}^{-1/2}_{i}&\mathsf{K}^{1/2}_{i}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\sinh(u-\zeta_i+h\HH_i/2)&\sinh(h)\exp(u-\zeta_i+h\HH_i/2)\mathsf{F}_{i}\\
\sinh(h)\mathsf{E}_{i}\exp(-u+\zeta_i-h\HH_i/2)&\sinh(u-\zeta_i-h\HH_i/2)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{a}_{i}&\mathsf{b}_{i}\\
\mathsf{c}_{i}&\mathsf{d}_{i}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathsf{H}_{i}}={\rm e}^{h\mathsf{H}_{i}}:=\mathsf{K}_{i}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{X}_{i},0}(u-h/2+\zeta_{i})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\rm e}^{u+\zeta_{i}}}{2}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{2}}-\dfrac{{\rm e}^{-u-\zeta_{i}}}{2}P_{\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{2}}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}_{i}}^{-1}\circ P_{\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\sinh(u+\zeta_i+h\HH_i/2)&\sinh(h)\mathsf{F}_{i}\exp(-u-\zeta_i+h\HH_i/2)\\
\sinh(h)\exp(u+\zeta_i-h\HH_i/2)\mathsf{E}_{i}&\sinh(u+\zeta_i-h\HH_i/2)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}&\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{i}\\
\hat{\mathsf{c}}_{i}&\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
C(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\hat{C}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=A(u)\hat{A}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\big(C(u)\hat{B}(u)+D(u)\hat{D}(u)\big)\ket{\mathbf{0}}.

(B.1)

Introducing a basis
\{\ket{\uparrow},\ket{\downarrow}\}
of the auxiliary space, we have, similarly to the one-boundary computation in Section
3.1
,

\displaystyle A(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\bra{\uparrow}T(u)\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{a}_{i}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sinh(u-\zeta_{i}+h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\ket{\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle\hat{A}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\bra{\uparrow}\hat{T}(u)\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sinh(u+\zeta_{i}+h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\ket{\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle D(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\bra{\downarrow}T(u)\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{i}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sinh(u-\zeta_{i}-h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\ket{\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle\hat{D}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\bra{\downarrow}\hat{T}(u)\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sinh(u+\zeta_{i}-h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\ket{\mathbf{0}}.

(B.2)

and

\displaystyle C(u)\hat{B}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle=\bra{\downarrow}T(u)\ket{\uparrow}\bra{\uparrow}\hat{T}(u)\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle=\bra{\downarrow}T(u)\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}\right)\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{k}\left(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\right)\ket{\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{i}\right)\mathsf{c}_{k}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathsf{a}_{i}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}\right)\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{k}\left(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\right)\ket{\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathsf{a}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}\right)\left(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\right)\mathsf{c}_{k}\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{k}\ket{\mathbf{0}}.

(B.3)

Note also that

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{k}\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sinh^{2}(h){\rm e}^{-2u}\mathsf{E}_{k}\mathsf{K}_{k}^{-1/2}\mathsf{F}_{k}\mathsf{K}_{k}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\sinh^{2}(h){\rm e}^{-2u+h}\mathsf{E}_{k}\mathsf{F}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm e}^{-2u+h}}{2}\left(\cosh(h\alpha_k)-\cosh(h\HH_k-h)\right)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-2u+h}\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_k+h\HH_k-h}{2})\sinh(\frac{h\alpha_k-h\HH_k+h}{2}),

(B.4)

where we used (
2.32
)–(
2.34
) to write

\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{X}_{k}}=4\sinh^{2}(h)\mathsf{E}_{k}\mathsf{F}_{k}+2\cosh(h\HH_k-h)=2\cosh(h\alpha_k).

Define

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{n}:={\rm e}^{-2u}\sinh(h)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{a}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}\right)+{\rm e}^{-h}\sinh(2u)\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathsf{a}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}\right)\left(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\right)\mathsf{c}_{k}\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{k}.

Let us show by induction on
n\geq 1
that
\mathsf{D}_{n}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=0
. For
n=1
, using (
B.4
), we can check by direct computation that

\mathsf{D}_{1}\ket{0_1}=\big({\rm e}^{-2u}\sinh(h)\big(\mathsf{d}_{1}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{1}-\mathsf{a}_{1}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{1}\big)+{\rm e}^{-h}\sinh(2u)\mathsf{c}_{1}\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{1}\big)\ket{0_1}=0.

(B.5)

Now assume that
\mathsf{D}_{n-1}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=0
. We have

\mathsf{a}_{n}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{n}\mathsf{D}_{n-1}=\mathsf{D}_{n}-\big({\rm e}^{-2u}\sinh(h)\big(\mathsf{d}_{n}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{n}-\mathsf{a}_{n}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{n}\big)+{\rm e}^{-h}\sinh(2u)\mathsf{c}_{n}\hat{\mathsf{b}}_{n}\big)\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathsf{d}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i},

so by (
B.5
) and the induction hypothesis
\mathsf{D}_{n}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=0
. By (
3.15
), (
B.1
)–(
B.3
),

\mathsf{D}_{n}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\sinh(2u-h)\left(\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\right)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=0,

so finally

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{a}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{a}}_{i}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sinh(u-\zeta_{i}+h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\sinh(u+\zeta_{i}+h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\ket{\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{i}\hat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\ket{\mathbf{0}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sinh(u-\zeta_{i}-h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\sinh(u+\zeta_{i}-h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2})\ket{\mathbf{0}}.

(B.6)

Therefore, using the commutation relations (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) and performing exactly the same computations (
3.29
) and (
3.30
) as in the one-boundary case but with the new eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}(u)
and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}(u)
(
B.6
), we find that

\ket{\{v_m\}}=\mathcal{B}(v_{1})\cdots\mathcal{B}(v_{M})\ket{\mathbf{0}}

is an eigenvector of the transfer matrix
t(u)
with eigenvalue

\displaystyle\Lambda(\{v_{m}\};u)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u)}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{\alpha_{i},\zeta_{i}}(u)\right)\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m-h)\sinh(u+v_m-h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sinh(2u-h)}{\sinh(2u)}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{\alpha_{i},\zeta_{i}}(-u)\right)\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\sinh(u-v_m+h)\sinh(u+v_m+h)}{\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)},

where

\Delta_{\alpha_{i},\zeta_{i}}(u):=\sinh(u+h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2}-\zeta_{i})\sinh(u+h\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{2}+\zeta_{i})

if and only if
\{v_{m}\}_{1\leq m\leq M}
satisfy the Bethe ansatz equations

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\Delta_{\alpha_{i},\zeta_{i}}(v_{m})}{\Delta_{\alpha_{i},\zeta_{i}}(-v_{m})}=\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{M}\frac{\sinh(v_m-v_k+h)\sinh(v_m+v_k+h)}{\sinh(v_m-v_k-h)\sinh(v_m+v_k-h)}

(B.7)

for all
1\leq m\leq M
. As before
\mathcal{B}(\infty):=\lim_{u\to+\infty}{\rm e}^{-2nu}\mathcal{B}(u)\propto\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{X}}
and so we expect the finite (permutation invariant) solutions
\{v_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq M}
of the BAE (
B.7
) belonging to the fundamental domain
S_{+}
to provide all the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
highest-weight eigenstates of weight
\mathfrak{q}^{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\right)-n-2M}
of
t(u)
.

Note that for
\mathcal{X}_{i}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, that is
\alpha_{i}=2
,
\Delta_{2,0}(u)=\sinh(u-h/2)^{2}
, and for
\mathcal{X}_{i}=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l/r}}
,
\Delta_{\alpha_{l/r},\zeta_{l/r}}(u)\propto\Delta_{l/r}(u)
(
3.58
). Thus for
n=N+2
,
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}
,
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{l}
,
\mathcal{X}_{N+2}=\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}
,
\zeta_{N+2}=\zeta_{r}
and
\mathcal{X}_{i}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
\zeta_{i}=0
for all
2\leq i\leq N+1
, we recover (
3.59
) and (
3.60
) (up to normalisation of
t_{2b}(u)
) as we should.

Appendix C
A different set of BAE for the two-boundary system

Following
[
60
]
, it is possible to find the general form of the eigenvalues of
t_{2b}(u)
solely from the knowledge of some functional relations it satisfies and their analytic properties. The end result is that for any eigenvalue
\Lambda_{2b}(\{v_{m}\};u)
of
t_{2b}(u)
one can write a TQ relation of the form

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2b}(\{v_{m}\};u)=

\displaystyle\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\Delta_{l}(u)\Delta_{r}(u)\dfrac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u)}\dfrac{Q(u-h)}{Q(u)}

\displaystyle+\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\Delta_{l}(-u)\Delta_{r}(-u)\dfrac{\sinh(2u-h)}{\sinh(2u)}\dfrac{Q(u+h)}{Q(u)}

\displaystyle+c\dfrac{\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\sinh(2u+h)\sinh(2u-h)}{Q(u)},

where

Q(u)=\prod_{m=1}^{N}\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)

for some Bethe roots
v_{m}
,
1\leq m\leq N
and
c
some constant. The Bethe ansatz equations are obtained by imposing that
\Lambda_{2b}(\{v_{m}\};u)
has no poles at all the
v_{m}
, that is

\displaystyle\sinh^{2N}(v_{m}+h/2)\Delta_{l}(v_{m})\Delta_{r}(v_{m})\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{N}\sinh(v_m-v_k-h)\sinh(v_m+v_k-h)

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sinh^{2N}(v_{m}-h/2)\Delta_{l}(-v_{m})\Delta_{r}(-v_{m})\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{N}\sinh(v_m-v_k+h)\sinh(v_m+v_k+h)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad{}=c\sinh^{2N}(v_{m}+h/2)\sinh^{2N}(v_{m}-h/2)

(C.1)

for all
1\leq m\leq N
. Note that if
c\neq 0
, the trigonometric polynomial
Q(u)
has to be of degree
2N
for the BAE (
C.1
) to admit at least one solution.

The right-hand side of (
C.1
) is known as the “inhomogeneous term” and this equation provides the BAE for
H_{{\rm n.d.}}
(
1.1
) for arbitrary values of
h
,
\delta_{r/l}
,
\kappa_{r/l}
and
\Theta
, whether they satisfy the Nepomechie condition or not, with
c
an explicit function of these parameters. Actually, the Nepomechie cases (
1.3
) with
0\leq M\leq N
correspond to
c=0
. Indeed, if
c=0
, then a subset of the
v_{m}
can be sent to
\infty
and we obtain

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2b}(\{v_{m}\};u)={}

\displaystyle\sinh^{2N}(u+h/2)\Delta_{l}(u)\Delta_{r}(u)\dfrac{\sinh(2u+h)}{\sinh(2u)}\dfrac{Q(u-h)}{Q(u)}

\displaystyle{}+\sinh^{2N}(u-h/2)\Delta_{l}(-u)\Delta_{r}(-u)\dfrac{\sinh(2u-h)}{\sinh(2u)}\dfrac{Q(u+h)}{Q(u)}

with

Q(u)=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\sinh(u-v_m)\sinh(u+v_m)

for some magnon number
0\leq M\leq N
as in (
3.59
) and the BAE (
C.1
) then reduce to the form (
3.60
)

\dfrac{\Delta_{l}(v_{m})\Delta_{r}(v_{m})}{\Delta_{l}(-v_{m})\Delta_{r}(-v_{m})}\left(\dfrac{\sinh(v_m+h/2)}{\sinh(v_m-h/2)}\right)^{2N}=\prod_{\underset{k\neq m}{k=1}}^{M}\frac{\sinh(v_m-v_k+h)\sinh(v_m+v_k+h)}{\sinh(v_m-v_k-h)\sinh(v_m+v_k-h)}

for all
1\leq m\leq M
. Thus the BAE from
[
60
]
are consistent with (
3.60
) as long as
0\leq M\leq N
. However, we have seen that in the two-boundary case the magnon number
M
can take any (positive) integer value. The question is then what happens for
M>N
.

For this we need to come back to the general
c\neq 0
case. It turns out that the constant
c
can be entirely fixed from the knowledge of the large
u
asymptotic. Indeed, if
c\neq 0
, then

\Lambda_{2b}(\{v_{m}\};u)\underset{u\to\infty}{\sim}4^{-N-2}{\rm e}^{2(N+2)u}\left(\dfrac{2\mu_{l}\mu_{r}\cosh(h\alpha_l+h\alpha_r-(N+1)h)}{\sinh(h)^{2}\sinh(h\alpha_l)\sinh(h\alpha_r)}+4c\right).

(C.2)

On the other hand, we can compute the large
u
limit of
t_{2b}(u)
directly. We have (
3.38
)

R_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}}(u)\underset{u\to\infty}{\sim}\dfrac{{\rm e}^{u+\frac{h}{2}}}{2}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{2}},\qquad R_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}(u)\underset{u\to\infty}{\sim}\dfrac{{\rm e}^{u+\frac{h}{2}}}{2}\mathsf{R}_{\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathcal{X}}

for any
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
module
\mathcal{X}
and so, using (
3.51
) and (
3.52
) and then (
3.48
)–(
3.50
)

\displaystyle t_{2b}(u)\underset{u\to\infty}{\sim}{}

\displaystyle\dfrac{4^{-N-2}{\rm e}^{2(N+2)u}\mu_{l}\mu_{r}\operatorname{qtr}_{0}\mathsf{R}_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}}}\mathsf{R}_{0,N}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{0,1}\mathsf{R}_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}},0}\mathsf{R}_{1,0}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{N,0}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{r}},0}}{\sinh(h)^{2}\sinh(h\alpha_l)\sinh(h\alpha_r)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\dfrac{4^{-N-2}{\rm e}^{2(N+2)u}\mu_{l}\mu_{r}\operatorname{qtr}_{0}\mathsf{R}_{0,\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b},0}}{\sinh(h)^{2}\sinh(h\alpha_l)\sinh(h\alpha_r)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\dfrac{4^{-N-2}{\rm e}^{2(N+2)u}\mu_{l}\mu_{r}\left(\mathfrak{q}\left(\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\{1\}^{2}\mathsf{F}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}\right)+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathsf{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}^{-1}\right)}{\sinh(h)^{2}\sinh(h\alpha_l)\sinh(h\alpha_r)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\dfrac{4^{-N-2}{\rm e}^{2(N+2)u}\mu_{l}\mu_{r}\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}}{\sinh(h)^{2}\sinh(h\alpha_l)\sinh(h\alpha_r)},

where
\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}
is the Casimir element (
2.32
) evaluated in the representation
{\mathcal{H}_{2b}}
. Recalling that
\mathsf{C}
is constant on any irreducible
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
module, in particular (
2.34
)

\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}}=\mathfrak{q}^{\alpha}+\mathfrak{q}^{-\alpha}=2\cosh(h\alpha)

and the
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
decomposition (
2.36
)

\mathcal{H}_{2b}=\bigoplus_{M\geq 0}\mathcal{H}_{M}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{l}+\alpha_{r}-1+N-2M}

it is then clear that

t_{2b}(u)|_{\mathcal{H}_{M}}\underset{u\to\infty}{\sim}4^{-N-2}{\rm e}^{2(N+2)u}\dfrac{2\mu_{l}\mu_{r}\cosh(h\alpha_l+h\alpha_r+h(N-2M-1))}{\sinh(h)^{2}\sinh(h\alpha_l)\sinh(h\alpha_r)}.

(C.3)

Now comparing (
C.2
) and (
C.3
), we finally arrive at

c=\dfrac{\mu_{l}\mu_{r}\sinh(h(N-M))\sinh(h\alpha_l+h\alpha_r-h(M+1))}{\sinh(h)^{2}\sinh(h\alpha_l)\sinh(h\alpha_r)}.

(C.4)

We are thus led to conjecture that for
M\geq N
, the BAE (
3.60
) on
M
Bethe roots are equivalent to the BAE (
C.1
) with only
N
Bethe roots and
c
as in (
C.4
). Note that the case
M=N
is trivial since
c
vanishes precisely for
M=N
.
